# Changelog

All notable changes to the Test Architect (TEA) module will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- A run with no `--partition` scores each probe against the oracle of its own partition (Story 1.110, AD-9, AD-22).
  The both view gives a behavior its development oracle and its held-out oracle, and eval-quality designates an oracle for a probe only when its behavior lists exactly one, so a both run scored every probe of such a behavior undesignated: a probe read `caught: true` only when a finding cited the contract's first-declared oracle and `caught: false` for a defect claimed against any other oracle.
  A probe now asks `eval-quality score --designated-oracle` for the one oracle its partition's own view lists for its behavior: the held-out view's oracle when `heldOutProbes` lists the probe, the development view's otherwise, derived by the same `contractView` the partition's own run compiles.
  A behavior whose partition view lists no oracle or several stays undesignated, as in the partition's own run, and a behavior the both view lists with one oracle is designated by the engine.
  A development run, a held-out run and a folder with no `partitionPlan` pass nothing, so every committed score, baseline and replay of them keeps its bytes, and a development run never opens the held-out plan.
  `HeldInputs` in `score-inputs.js` is the one place that reads the designation: `scoreArguments` adds the flag and `reproduce` hands the same oracle to the in-process re-score, so the CLI call and the held-input check cannot disagree, and `score`, `compare --accept`, `ci` and an evaluator qualification attempt all go through it.
  `score` reads the folder's `contract.json` and held-out plan for a both run, and refuses with an input-check finding (exit 10, no score call) a plan, `evaluation.json` or `contract.json` that cannot be read, a probe whose behavior the contract does not hold, and a folder whose both view lists other oracles under a probe's behavior than the contract the run sealed, whether or not an oracle is designated (a plan that dropped or added an oracle, a `contract.json` that gained one, a `partitionPlan` that was removed).
  A finding names the behavior by an ID of the schema's shape and quotes no byte of the plan.
  `compare --accept` reports the same findings, so a both run whose folder drifted never becomes a baseline.
  The `partition-plan` fixture's both run now scores P-003 against O-101 and P-004 against O-002 and catches both, as the held-out and development runs do; a both baseline accepted with `compare --accept` replays through `ci --tier pr` with every probe as recorded and no stale warning; a development clean control on a behavior with several oracles stays undesignated and reads as it does in the development run; a call that drops the flag is refused as a staged artifact that differs from the held inputs'; and the gameability probes of a both run are designated too.
  A gameability probe's `defectSignature` must admit and be satisfied by the answer at every step an oracle of either partition reads, because the both view answers every step and eval-quality reads a finding at a step the signature does not admit as an unwitnessed claim.
  The reference and the corpus guide say so, and the fixture's development gameability probe selects any prompt.
- `test:doc-counts` holds the replay totals, the story count and the five lane lists of the Evaluate plan to the files that own them (Story 1.95, CAP-12).
  The totals that `test/README.md` and the header of `test/test-eval-replay.js` state (the cases, the cases that produce a number, the constructed ones among them, the cases that carry captured bytes and where they come from, and the trace, `nfr` and `ci` case counts) equal the counts read from the `expected.json` files under `test/replay/`, so a replay case added without moving them fails.
  The overview of `epics.md` states its story count (every `### Story` section, H.1 included), its epic count and the number of appended stories, and its Parallel lanes section states the lane count; the gate holds each to the story sections and lane lists the file holds.
  The module `test/lib/planning-doc-sources.js` refuses a plan whose two appended-story lists do not name exactly the Epic 1 stories numbered 1.27 and up, and a plan whose five lane lists in `epics.md` and `sprint-status.yaml` differ in membership or order, hold a story twice or in two lanes, miss a lane, or name an entry with no status row.
  `test:planning-doc-sources` observes every gate failing on data changed by one: a count off by one, an entry removed from each of the five lanes in each file, a story moved to another lane or duplicated in one file only, an order swap, a lane missing from either file, and a stored replay case added.
- The evaluation layer's processes have no route to the host's path-based Unix sockets (Story 1.88, AD-7, AD-8).
  The processes that run your code or an agent for the evaluation (a `command` evaluator, a sealed-brief agent and the bridge relay it starts, the rubric judge and the evaluation's HTTP port) kept every socket of the host, so a sealed-brief agent run by a user in the `docker` group could start a container that writes the evaluation folder.
  Under Bubblewrap each start of such a process now lists the host's sockets again, the list a target's call reads, and binds each socket's own path onto itself and then an empty device file over it, so `/var/run/docker.sock`, the system bus and an agent socket under `/tmp` answer `ECONNREFUSED`.
  The layer's `/` is a writable bind of the host's, where a mount over a path that went away makes Bubblewrap create an empty file there.
  A socket that went away before Bubblewrap starts stops the start and makes no file, since Bubblewrap checks every bind source before any mount.
  A socket its owner removes while Bubblewrap mounts it can leave an empty file and its directory chain at that path for as long as the layer process runs, and the file is no route to anything.
  The runtime removes what Bubblewrap made when the process has ended, and the next run removes it for a run that was killed first.
  It records the state of each hidden path before every start and acts only on an empty regular file with no write bit that its user owns and that appeared after the start, and on the empty directories Bubblewrap made again; a socket the owner made again, a file with content and every other file stay.
  A layer process cannot write a record of the guard: the macOS profile denies every write to the private root and to each entry directly in it, and every Bubblewrap vector binds the root read-only with the run's own private parents writable, whatever the number of sockets it hides.
  A record from a process the layer does not confine acts only on empty files with no write bit and empty directories that its user owns and that were born after the record, and the next run deletes a record that another user owns, that has a mode other than 0600, whose pid differs from its name or whose start is far from its own change time, which refuses a backdated record.
  `references/run.md` and `references/gaps.md` state that a socket a host process binds after a confined call or layer process started is reachable wherever nothing masks or denies its path, since seccomp cannot read a socket's path, a network namespace does not scope path sockets and AppArmor needs a profile that root loads.
  The socket the runtime serves, the bridge a sealed-brief agent's relay connects to, stays connectable, and a host socket another process moves into the private root `/tmp/tea-evaluate-p<uid>` stays hidden.
  A start whose list does not fit the room its command leaves is refused, since the layer has no record that could name a socket left reachable, and a socket bound after the process started stays reachable under Bubblewrap.
  The macOS layer profile carries the denial of Story 1.87 and allows back the bridge's socket shape beneath the private root and the two system services, so a socket bound after the process started is refused there, with `EPERM`.
  It also denies every write to the private root, to each entry directly in it and to the bridge's directory, so a layer process cannot rename a host directory or a whole tree that holds a socket (Docker Desktop's `~/.docker/run`, the directory `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` names) to a path the allowance names and connect there.
  A layer that cannot hide the host's sockets ends the command with exit 12 and the reason alone, and the HTTP port reads Bubblewrap's two messages for a socket that went away as a host condition, exit 12, when the message opens the process's standard error, the status is 1 and the port had not answered; a port that prints the same text itself keeps exit 10.
  `the layer path socket units` and `the layer path socket guard` run a stand-in for Bubblewrap that follows its order over the real file system, on every host, and read the host directory after each spawn site.
  `the layer path socket recovery` kills a run that launched through the stand-in with the race staged and recovers it.
  `the layer path socket route` runs the real Bubblewrap and reads the directory afterwards in the ubuntu CI job.
  `the Seatbelt layer socket units` and `the Seatbelt layer socket route` hold the macOS profile, the rename included.
  The reference's sentence that said the layer keeps every socket of the host is replaced by ten sentences on what the layer's processes reach and what they do not, and the isolation golden gains the layer's vector with sockets and the rules in the Seatbelt layer profile.
- A macOS Seatbelt target has no route to the host's path-based Unix sockets (Story 1.87, AD-8).
  The target profile started from `(allow default)` and refused a `connect()` to a socket under the user's private root only, so a confined process could connect to `/var/run/docker.sock`, Docker Desktop's `~/.docker/run/docker.sock`, the socket `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` names or an agent socket under `/private/tmp` and ask the service behind it to run a job outside the sandbox.
  The profile now denies every `connect()` to a socket path and allows back the workspace, the call's private directories, the home and two system services: the resolver at `/var/run/mDNSResponder`, which every name lookup of the C library asks, and the BSD log socket at `/var/run/syslog`.
  Every other socket of `/var/run` stays closed, the privileged helper of Docker Desktop among them.
  The rule names a path, so a socket a host process binds after the call started is refused too, which Bubblewrap's mounts cannot do.
  A connection answers `EPERM`, and a link to a socket outside the grants is refused since Seatbelt matches the socket's real path.
  `the Seatbelt path socket route` connects from a real Seatbelt call to a socket under the temp directory, links to it, the host's Docker sockets and the one `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` names, and to a socket bound after the call started, and finds each refused, then finds each reached with the denial taken out of the profile; it connects to a socket in the workspace, in a private directory and in the home, resolves the host's own `.local` name, and fails both under a profile that denies every socket.
  `the Seatbelt path socket units` read the profile's text on every host.
  The reference's macOS sentence on host sockets names what the profile closes and what it reaches, and the isolation golden gains the rule in its eight Seatbelt target profiles.
  The run guide and the gaps guide name both answers a confined target gets from a socket file of the host (Bubblewrap `ECONNREFUSED`, macOS Seatbelt `EPERM`), and `test:evaluate-guidance` fails when either guide names Bubblewrap alone.
- `tea-evaluate check` and `compare` verify the baseline's file digests (Story 1.90, AD-12).
  `compare --accept` records a `files` map of every baseline file's `digestBytes` in `baseline/baseline.json`, and nothing read it back, so a baseline file edited by hand passed both commands.
  `check` now reports a `baseline-digest` finding and exits 10 for each file of `baseline/` whose bytes differ from the map, each listed file that is missing and each file other than `baseline.json` that the map does not list.
  `check` also reports the finding when the manifest cannot be read, when it lists a path outside `baseline/` or one the file system cannot examine, and when `baseline/` holds anything but authored qualification evidence (`probes/`, `qualification/`, a placeholder `README.md`) with no manifest.
  A plain `compare` refuses a baseline that fails the same check with exit 10 and no verdict, before it reads any of the baseline's evidence.
  One module, `cli/lib/evaluate/baseline-digests.js`, holds the verification for both commands.
  A `test:evaluate-check` case and a `test:evaluate-compare` case accept a baseline through the real CLI, pass it, and fail it for a one-byte edit, a missing listed file and an unlisted file.
  The reference lists the `baseline-digest` rule in its `check` rule list and its `compare` section, and a static test fails when either mention is removed.
- A check of the CI plan can name the existing publish or deploy jobs its tier must gate, and `bmad-testarch-ci` makes each wait for the tier's evaluation job (Story 1.97, AD-10, AD-11, CAP-11).
  A `scheduled` or `release` evaluation job gated nothing unless the repository's own publish or deploy job waited for it, and the CI skill could not change a job it did not write.
  The plan gains an optional `gates` list on a check, the ids of the jobs its tier gates, and the jobs a tier gates are the union of `gates` over the checks the plan places on it.
  The schema holds the shape (a non-empty list of distinct job ids), and `tea-evaluate check` and `ci` report an empty list, a repeated name or a name that is no job id as a `ci-plan` finding of rule `gates` that exits 10.
  The ci stage of the Evaluate skill sets `gates` when its release flow inspection finds a publish or deploy job the tier must gate, and leaves it out otherwise; its first worked plan gates the `publish` job of `release.yml`.
  Step 03b renders the wait by where the job lives.
  Inside the pipeline file it writes, the evaluation job's id is appended to the job's `needs` with the entries it had kept, and nothing else of the job changes.
  In another workflow file the job gains an `if:` that requires a successful run of the pipeline file on the tier's event, its checkout steps name the evaluated commit, and its workflow gains a `workflow_run` trigger on the pipeline and an event guard on its other jobs.
  The step reports the edit in its summary, writes the wait again under the evaluation job id the rules give on a re-render, and removes it when the plan stops naming the job.
  A name that matches no job, or a gate that cannot hold, refuses the plan: the summary reports it and nothing renders from that plan.
  Inside the pipeline file a gate cannot hold when the gated job already ran in a run where its tier's evaluation job is skipped, when the tier's evaluation job runs on a run the job did not run on before (a `workflow_dispatch` the plan adds to a tag-push release file), when the job already waits for another plan's evaluation job through the cross-file form, or when its `if:` calls a status function other than `success()`; a run the render adds that the evaluation job skips is skipped through the wait and is no conflict.
  Across files a gate also cannot hold for a pull request tier, a `pull_request_target` tier or another event that runs code of a fork, for an evaluation job behind a ref or cron guard, for a gated workflow that already follows another workflow, for a job gated by another plan or by a second tier, and for a job with `needs`, a `uses:` job, an `if:` of its own or `github.ref`, `github.ref_name`, `github.sha`, `github.head_ref` or `github.event.*` contexts.
  Item 7 of the step now gives `workflow_dispatch` to every tier whose `trigger` names `manual-dispatch`, and guards the jobs that existed before when a render adds an event or widens the branch filters, tag patterns or crons of one the workflow has, an in-file gated job excepted.
  The Evaluate skill's path text (`SKILL.md`, `references/adapters.md`, `gaps.md`, `harness.md` and `run.md`) names a directory, a file or a command where it gave a machine path, so the builder's path scan reports nothing.
  The summary line for a tier wired to no event of its own stays, now without the reference to this story, because `gates` names a job and creates no event.
  `test:evaluate-ci` holds the field in the case `the gated jobs`, `test:evaluate-ci-render` pins the step's sentences, parses the template's gate block and scores one edit of the stored live capture per way a wait can go wrong, and `test:evaluate-guidance` holds the new section of `references/ci.md` with a corrupted guide for each sentence.
  The `ci` behavioral suite gains the `evaluation-gate` adopter, an edit set whose publish job waits for the test job and whose plan gates it on the release tier, a `wait` element in the harness, a stored live capture and two constructed deviations derived from it by one edit each (the entry cut from `needs`, and the release evaluation job limited to pull requests).
  The live capture scored 15 of 15 elements.
  Both ci-repos capture sessions ran again against the final guide through the local Claude Code CLI, so each `capture-record.json` pins the new digests of `ci.md` and `SKILL.md`.
  Both sessions set `gates` on their release `twin-run` check (`publish` for `tagged-release`, `production` for `nightly-deploy`).
  The ci stage now names every event that starts the gated job's workflow in the `trigger` of the gating tier's checks, so a nightly `deploy.yml` that also starts on `workflow_dispatch` lists `manual-dispatch` beside `release`.
  Step 03b section 2 refuses a gate whose job already ran on a dispatch the release evaluation job skips, and a gate whose evaluation job runs on a dispatch a tag-push release file did not have; `test:evaluate-ci-render` pins both shapes and renders the committed `nightly-deploy` plan when it names `manual-dispatch` on its release checks.
  The committed-plan shape check of `test:evaluate-ci` accepts any non-empty set of the events a tier uses.
  The recall threshold moves from 0.96 to 0.97 so that the larger corpus still admits two misses, the contract and probe corpora and `expected-strength.json` regenerate, and the `ci` suite lists six cases.
- TeA's own pull requests run the `pr` tier of every evaluation it keeps (Story 2.5, AD-11, CAP-11, CAP-12).
  Each of the nine fixture evaluations (the stdio MCP and HTTP API graders, the workflow, tool-use, promptfoo and learned-framework evaluations, the AI-feature and test-review authoring suites, and the gap-loop `after` evaluation) and the Evaluate-authored suite is a script of the `npm test` chain, `test:evaluate-pr-<key>`, which the `chain` matrix runs.
  `test/test-evaluate-pr-tier.js <key>` runs `tea-evaluate ci --tier pr` over the committed folder with no secret and no model call, and fails when the folder's plan omits a `pr` check its probes or interface call for (the gameability arm, the HTTP port conformance), when its baseline was recorded on another eval-quality release than the installed one, holds a dirty or incomplete run, a non-copy workspace, a manifest `acceptedRun` that names another run, or a partition other than both, or when the evaluation's `evaluation.json` declares a non-copy workspace, or when `ci` exits non-zero, runs other checks than the plan places or reads a stale baseline.
  Every fixture evaluation now commits `ci/evaluation-ci-plan.json` and a `baseline/`, so the replay, the gameability arm, contract-source freshness and oracle agreement run on every pull request.
  The suite runs `check`, `compile` and `seal` from a committed plan; its replay joins them when Story H.1 accepts its clean baseline.
  The `chain` job uploads each evaluation's `runs/<invocationId>/` as the build artifact `evaluate-runs-<shard>` with `if: always()`, so a red run leaves its evidence bundle.
  A `test:evaluate-ci` case parses `quality.yaml` and fails when the upload step, its `always()`, its shard-qualified name or any evaluation's path is missing, when a script leaves the `npm test` chain, and when any of the eight `eval-quality-gates` leaves its job: `test:lockfile-age` and `test:licences` stay in `supply-chain`, `test:direction`, `test:boundary` and `test:lineage` in `layering-boundary-lineage`, and `test:doc-invocations`, `test:doc-counts` and `test:doc-claims` in the chain.
  The case also carries a revert case for each folder rule, each wiring rule and each result rule.
- A gameability probe runs under a `partitionPlan`, and its arm answers only the steps of its own view (Story 1.109, AD-9, AD-22).
  Story 1.51 refused a gameability probe beside a `partitionPlan`, because the degenerate response answered every plan step from one file and could not name a held-out step in a development file.
  The response now splits where the plan does.
  `corpus/gameability/<probeId>.json` answers the steps of `contract.json`, the shared and the development-only ones, and `corpus/held-out/gameability/<probeId>.json`, sealed beside the held-out plan, answers the steps of the plan in the same shape, so no development file holds a held-out step ID.
  `preflight` and `run` answer each arm from the steps its view declares (`answersForView`): the development view reads the first file, the held-out view takes the shared steps from the first and its own from the second, and the both view takes every step from both.
  Every gameability probe answers every step of the plan, because the both view runs each probe over the whole plan whichever partition the probe belongs to.
  A development `preflight` and `run` read no byte of `corpus/held-out/`, the held-out plan and the held-out answers alike, so an answers file a development run cannot read stops only a held-out or both run and `check`.
  The development corpus-index comparison leaves the whole `corpus/held-out/` directory out: it decides each entry by its path before any `lstat` or listing, so it neither lists nor opens what the directory holds, and the adopter-tree reading that brackets a run lists the directory and takes only the `lstat` size, modification time and mode of what it holds, with the error code of a path `lstat` cannot reach.
  So a link, a FIFO or a path nobody may open or enter anywhere under `corpus/held-out/`, in the answers, the plan or a file beside them, stops only a held-out or both run and `check`, which refuse it by path with exit 10 and no stack.
  `check` and a held-out or both run refuse a directory spelled in another case (`corpus/held-out/Gameability/`, or `corpus/Held-Out/` for the plan) by path on every file system, because both readers compare the native real path, which spells the directory as the disk does; a development run seals the other spelling like the sealed one, in its comparison and in its tree reading.
  The qualification and trial evidence of a held-out or both run records the held-out answers' path and digest beside the response file's, and a development run's record is the response file alone.
  `check` holds the held-out answers to the rules of the response file over the held-out plan's steps (required when the plan declares a step) and names a missing, misplaced or unreadable answer by probe and step ID, naming a held-out step by its ID only when it has the schema's shape and never quoting the sealed file.
  A held-out probe's `naiveOracle` that reads a development-only step is a finding, because the held-out view drops it.
  The `partition-plan` refusal of a gameability probe is gone.
  Making the arm answer the whole plan puts a held-out step in a development artifact and fails the isolation case, a development run that opens the held-out answers fails the case that makes them unreadable, and a missing answer that passes `check` fails its case.
  With no `partitionPlan`, every committed fixture, baseline and replay is byte-identical.
- The Linux audit lists a connection to a Unix socket file outside the grants as an observed mount (Story 1.86, AD-7, AD-8).
  Story 1.82 mounts an empty device file over each path-based socket the host lists when a call starts, and a socket a host process binds after the call started stayed reachable and unlisted, because the audit traced file syscalls and a `connect()` is none.
  The trace now also reports `connect` and the sends that carry an address (`sendto`, `sendmsg`, `sendmmsg`), so a datagram socket is covered as a stream socket is.
  A call the kernel did not refuse is listed by the socket file's real path (a link in the workspace to the socket resolves to the file the kernel reached), and `score` exits 3 with eval-quality's isolation violation naming it.
  A connection the mounts refused (`ECONNREFUSED`), a path with nothing behind it, a socket file the process may not open, an abstract address, a TCP or UDP address and a send with no address list nothing.
  A socket in the workspace, in a private directory or the home of the call (the bridge's included), in the sandbox's own `/dev` and `/run/user`, or the egress proxy's directory is the call's own and lists nothing.
  A call the end of the call cut short (strace prints its return value as `= ?`) is listed, and so is a connection through a link the target made and removed afterwards, whether the link was copied by a hard link, moved with its directory, exchanged by `RENAME_EXCHANGE`, made through a link to a directory or made in the sandbox's own `/dev`.
  A connection through a path the target removes, renames or replaces afterwards, or whose directory it does, is listed by the path as given unless the call bound that socket itself and its name has stood since the bind, because a link your project held and the target removed leaves the host nothing to read.
  A bind mount whose source lies outside the grants (`mount`, `open_tree` and `move_mount` are traced) is listed by its source, whatever the source holds, so a target that runs a nested sandbox which bind-mounts system paths gets `score` exit 3, and `strace` fails `io_uring_setup` with `ENOSYS`, so no `io_uring` request carries a connection past the trace.
  A connection through the destination of a bind mount is judged by the source it leads to, whichever spelling of the destination's directory the connection uses, and a `..` after a link in the path of a connection goes to the parent of the directory the link leads to.
  A link a `..` passed that the target then removes, renames or replaces lists the connection by the path as given, and a socket the call bound under one spelling of its directory is the call's own only while neither spelling was replaced after the bind.
  A call that names no Unix socket file holds nothing in the reader, whether it finished or another process's output split it, so a target that makes a million `send` calls costs no memory.
  The reader replays link changes and removals in time linear in the trace, a rename of a directory that holds tens of thousands of links included, so a target that makes tens of thousands of links, renames and removals adds seconds to the audit.
  The CLI reference's `### File-system confinement` names the connection in the audit passage and the sentence on a late socket points to it.
  `test:evaluate-confinement` runs `the socket connection units` on every host over trace lines written by hand in strace 6's spelling, and the Linux cases (`the socket connection route`, `the socket connection run`) run in the ubuntu CI job and are skipped elsewhere with their reason named.
- An evaluator mapping follows the contract view under a `partitionPlan` (Story 1.107, AD-9, AD-21, AD-22).
  Story 1.51 refused a `partitionPlan` beside any evaluator but the deterministic one, because a `command` or `sealed-brief-agent` evaluator binds oracles and rubric criteria through `evaluator/mapping.json` and a `records` evaluator's records name them too.
  A command evaluator, a sealed-brief agent and a records evaluator now run beside a plan, and the refusal is gone.
  A row of `evaluator/mapping.json` binds what `contract.json` declares: it is in the development and both views, and in the held-out view unless the held-out view drops the oracle or criterion it binds.
  The held-out plan's optional `mappings` array (`held-out-plan.schema.json`, `schemaVersion` stays 1) holds the rows for what only the held-out partition declares, `{ key, oracleId, behaviorId }` or `{ key, rubricId, criterionId, levels }`, and they join the held-out and both views only, so no held-out ID sits in a file the development run reads.
  `mappingView` derives each partition's mapping beside `contractView`: the development view is the file's own bytes, the held-out view is always serialized so its bytes move only with the rows it holds, and the both view of a plan with no `mappings` is the file's bytes.
  A plan row never reuses the key of a row `evaluator/mapping.json` declares, whether or not the held-out view kept that row.
  A run's evaluator layer holds its view's mapping only: the rows its answers are validated against and converted by, the keys a sealed-brief agent is shown, and `tea.evaluatorTreeDigest`, which digests the view's mapping in place of the file, so a development run's digest depends on no held-out row.
  A command evaluator receives `{ sealedBrief, observations }` and never the mapping, so its input follows the view through the brief and the observations.
  The development partition reads, executes and digests the files under `evaluator/`, so a command evaluator derives a held-out key from its input and spells none.
  A `records` evaluator reads no mapping, and eval-quality's `score` ignores an oracle its contract lacks and leaves the rubric criterion check to the caller, so under a `partitionPlan` a record that carries an oracle disposition, a finding's oracle or behavior, a judge result, an observation or a citation the run's view does not hold is refused with exit 10 and nothing copied, named by where it sits in the record (`oracleDispositions[2]`, `observations[3]`, `findings[1].observationIds[0]`) and never by the ID.
  An observation is admitted only when its ID is `<run label>-<a step the run's view declares>` or `<run label>-call-<n>`, with the run labels `trial-<n>`, `attempt-<n>`, `baseline`, `degenerate`, `mutated` and `re-pass-<n>`, because a development run never opens the plan and cannot know a held-out step ID.
  A citation is admitted only when its record holds the observation it names.
  `check` refuses a step named `call-<n>` under a `partitionPlan`, in `contract.json` or in the held-out plan, because its observation would look like an agent's chosen call.
  `check` refuses a records evaluator beside a rubric and a `partitionPlan`, because the harness's calibration judgments answer one labelled file for one contract, a development `check`, `preflight` and `run` included whenever `contract.json` declares the rubric, and refuses `mappings` beside an evaluator that reads none.
  `check` names a plan row by its place in `mappings` and never by its key: a key `evaluator/mapping.json` or an earlier row has, an oracle, behavior or criterion the held-out view does not declare or another key binds, levels other than the criterion's anchored scale levels (the levels themselves stay out of the finding), and a held-out plan criterion no key binds.
  A row of `evaluator/mapping.json` that binds an oracle or criterion `contract.json` lacks is named by its key and IDs, with where the row belongs, by `check` of every partition.
  The mapping rules run over the held-out view only when the plan reads, passes its schema and makes a view the engine's contract schema accepts, so a contract error elsewhere is the only finding about it.
  Removing the held-out view's mapping filter fails the pure view case and the held-out run at the evaluator layer, and a held-out row in `evaluator/mapping.json` stops a development `check`, `preflight` and `run` with exit 10, no launch and a finding that says the row belongs in the plan's `mappings`.
  `test:evaluate-partition-plans` runs each partition under a command evaluator over split oracle and criterion rows, scans its recorded stdin, its run directory and the evaluator files a development run reads, runs a sealed-brief agent through each partition and scans its prompts, and imports records with foreign content, observations of the other partition's step included, in a development and a held-out run.
- `tea-evaluate digest --evaluation <folder> --file <path>` prints the digest of one file the folder holds (Story 1.115, CAP-4, AD-5).
  It prints eval-quality's `digestBytes` over the file's bytes, `sha256:` and 64 hex digits on one line, and writes nothing under the folder, `corpus-index.json` included.
  The path is relative to the evaluation folder; an absolute path, a `..` segment, a symbolic link at any component, a directory, a missing file, a path that cannot be read, a file that changed while it was read and anything that is not a regular file each exit 64 with one line on stderr, and so does `--file` with `--calibration-inputs`.
  The `intake.md` and `contract.md` guides and the assets README name the command where they say to stamp `requirements.digest` and `sourceSpecDigest`, and `intake.md` no longer carries an inline `node --input-type=module -e` script for it.
  `test:evaluate-check` holds the printed digest, the changed byte, the refused paths and the empty write set, and `test:evaluate-guidance` holds the sentence in each guide and runs the command as the guide spells it.
- The `ci` behavioral suite proves the merge, scheduled and release pipelines of an evaluation plan, their re-render and the CI skill's edit mode (Story 1.93, AD-11, AD-12).
  Story 2.3 proved one slice of `steps-c/step-03b-render-evaluation-plans.md`: a plan with checks on the `pr` tier, rendered in create mode.
  Two fixture adopters join `test/fixtures/ci-eval/`.
  The `evaluation-tiers` adopter places checks on `pr`, `merge`, `scheduled` and `release`, and its request never names the plan.
  Its ground truth lists the four triggers (`release` of type `published` among them), a job per tier with its one `tea-evaluate ci` step named for the tier's checks, the `merge` job's `pr` step ahead of its own, each job limited to the event that starts its tier, the test job limited to the events it already ran on, the 30, 30, 120 and 120 minute limits, and each job's `runs/` uploaded under `if: always()` as an artifact named for its job.
  The `evaluation-edit` adopter is the first edit-mode set: its pipeline holds a hand-written job and an evaluation job under the plan's marker, and the second run has to replace the marker job under the id the rules give now, leave the hand-written job byte for byte and leave the create run's checkpoint untouched.
  The harness gains the `mode`, `editTarget`, `preserved` and `checkpoint` set fields, the `job` fields `after`, `runsOn`, `timeoutMinutes` and `markerJobs`, the `artifact` fields `jobId` and `name`, the `command` field `runsOn` and the `trigger` field `types`, refuses a tier job that runs another tier's step, reads a job's `needs` and the guards `github.event.schedule` and `startsWith(github.ref, ...)`, and has an edit prompt that chooses `[E] Edit`.
  A live `eval:ci` run of each adopter scored every requested element with no unrequested element, twice over, and its workflow is stored as a real capture under `test/replay/ci/` beside twelve constructed deviations, three of which are the ones the story names: a `merge` job without the `pr` step, one artifact name for two jobs and a `scheduled` job under the `pr` timeout.
  The recall threshold moves from 0.9 to 0.96 so that the larger corpus still admits two misses, the contract and probe corpora and `expected-strength.json` regenerate, and the `ci` suite lists five cases.
- A confined agent target authenticates with the login of its Claude Code subscription (Story 1.113, CAP-6, AD-7).
  A confined target runs with a private home, so a confined `tea-skill-runner --agent claude` call found no login and exited 4 on every request, and the reference told an operator to pass an API key variable.
  A command entry in `evaluation.json` now declares `"login": "claude"`, and the runtime hands its processes the logins Claude Code documents and nothing else of the user's home.
  The variable `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` passes when the host sets it (a token from `claude setup-token`), and no other credential variable does.
  The file `.credentials.json` in `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, or in `.claude` under the home, which Linux and Windows hosts keep the login in, is linked into each private home at `.claude/.credentials.json`, read-only, and neither its read nor a refused write is an observed mount.
  A second file under the real home that a target reads, in the configuration directory or beside it, is still an observed mount and `score` exits 3.
  `run.json` records each grant under `logins` by the variable's name and the file's path, the isolation manifest names the file read-only in `allowedMounts` and both sources in its forbidden-input note, and the run replaces the token's value and each string of eight characters or more in the file with `[redacted]` wherever any target prints it, a command, a tool server or an HTTP server.
  The file's `scopes`, `subscriptionType` and `rateLimitTier` values are no secret and stay as written, and every other field of the file is replaced.
  The run keeps every string the file has held since it began and reads the file again when each call settles, so a token Claude Code on the host rotates out of the file during a call is replaced along with the one that took its place (Story 1.113 review round 2).
  A read caught in the middle of a rewrite, which is not valid JSON, contributes its whole text, each whitespace-separated token and each quoted string, except the values under the public fields, which stay as written in a torn read as in a whole one.
  An opted-out run replaces the strings of the host's credentials file in every record as well, since a target that keeps the host's `HOME` reads the file there, and `run.json` records no file for it.
  A login held in the macOS Keychain has no grant, since no Seatbelt rule scopes the keychain to one item and the CLI finds it through the `HOME` a confined target holds privately.
  A confined run on a host with neither the file nor the variable exits 12 before any target starts, naming the token route and the opt-out, and the `tea-evaluate` reference documents both under "A subscription login under confinement".
  `test:evaluate-confinement` holds the file, the second-file, the token, the record, the refusal and the keychain cases over a stub CLI with a fake login, and `test:evaluate-preflight` holds the real runner and adapter.
- `tea-evaluate check` quotes eval-quality's refusal of a contract whose interfaces share an identifier (Story 1.102, CAP-4, AD-1, AD-5). An interface's `logicalId` is half of an operation's identity, and eval-quality 7.0.1 refuses two interfaces with one identifier at `compile` and `preflight` under the new `duplicate-interface-identifier` code, naming the identifier and both interface positions. Before it, `compile` accepted the contract and `seal` faulted on it as an anonymous `schema-parse-failure`. For a contract of two or more interfaces `check` asks the engine's compile once, shared with the report-collision rule, and prints the engine's line as one `interface-identifier` finding on `contract.json`, so the repeat exits 10 before `seal` or the run's compile. TeA compares no identifier. The peer floor, `tools/guard-publish.js`, `test/test-release-metadata.js`, the lockfile and the AD-5 record move to `>=7.0.1`, and the accepted baselines of the mutation, MCP and API fixtures are re-recorded on it.
- A rubric criterion belongs to one partition under a `partitionPlan` (Story 1.105, AD-9, AD-22).
  Story 1.51 refused a `partitionPlan` beside a rubric that reads a development-only step, because the criterion's evidence pointer left with its step in the held-out view.
  A criterion now belongs to the partition whose steps its `evidence` pointer reads.
  A criterion in `contract.json` is in the development view, and in the held-out view unless it reads a development-only step; a rubric the held-out view leaves with no criterion leaves with them.
  The held-out plan's optional `rubrics` array (`held-out-plan.schema.json`, `schemaVersion` stays 1) holds the criteria only the held-out partition judges, under rubric IDs `contract.json` does not use, and they join the held-out and both views only.
  The development view stays the folder's own `contract.json` bytes and never holds a plan criterion; a plan with no `rubrics` leaves every view byte for byte as it was.
  Each partition's run compiles, calibrates and scores its own view's criteria.
  `policy/judge-calibration.json` stays one file, and a partition's run judges the items of the criteria its view holds, so an item of the other partition's criterion is not judged there; `check`, which reads both files, still names an item of a criterion no view declares.
  `check` names a criterion that no view can reach by its criterion and rubric IDs (a `contract.json` criterion reading a step the development view lacks, a plan criterion reading a development-only step or a step no view holds), a plan rubric ID that collides with `contract.json`'s or repeats, and a judge block no view uses.
  `check` keeps the plan's text out of every finding: it names a plan criterion by `rubricId/criterionId` only when both IDs have the schema's shape and by its position in the plan's `rubrics` otherwise, and it never prints a plan evidence pointer or scale level.
  The rubric, judge and labelled-file rules run over the both view only when the plan file reads, passes its schema and the partition-plan rules, and makes a held-out view the engine's contract schema accepts; otherwise they hold the rubrics `check` can see, so a `contract.json` that fails its own schema is the only finding about it.
  A judge finding over the both view says "the contract and its held-out plan declare a rubric" and prints no count, since `contract.json` alone may declare none.
  Removing the held-out view's partition filter fails the pure view case, and a held-out run over a view that keeps a development criterion stops at the engine's compile (`rubric-evidence-unreachable`, exit 4) before the run-level isolation scan.
  The `corpus.md` and `gaps.md` guides name the labelled items of a plan criterion as closed to the authoring loop with the plan, and `corpus.md` asks for one item at every anchored level, which is what the calibration rule needs.
  A contract whose only rubrics sit in the held-out plan still declares the judge once in `evaluation.json`, a development run over it calls no judge, and `ci`'s `judge-calibration` check calibrates the view of the baseline's partition.
  Story 1.106 partitions waivers by the same rule.
  The `corpus.md` guide teaches the rule with a tagged `held-out-rubrics` example that `test:evaluate-guidance` validates through the plan schema, `check` and the engine's contract schema, and the `tea-evaluate` reference and the AD-22 text record it.
  `test:evaluate-partition-plans` and `test:evaluate-calibration` hold each view's criteria, the unreachable-criterion findings and the per-partition calibration.
- A waiver belongs to one partition under a `partitionPlan` (Story 1.106, AD-9, AD-22).
  Story 1.51 refused a `partitionPlan` beside a waiver that reads a development-only step.
  eval-quality's `Waiver` holds `id`, `rule`, `rationale`, `condition`, `approval` and `expiresAt`: it names a discipline rule and no oracle, so its `condition`, the one field that reads a step, places it.
  A waiver in `contract.json` is in the development view, and in the held-out view unless its condition reads a development-only step; a waiver whose condition reads no step is in every view.
  A waiver's `condition` is free text, so every `/interactions/<stepId>` pointer in the sentence names a step, wherever it sits and with or without a path after the step; a waiver that names a development-only step anywhere, beside a shared step or alone, leaves the held-out view, and the pointers of `pointer`, `captured` and `evidence` stay anchored to the start of their string.
  The held-out plan's optional `waivers` array (`held-out-plan.schema.json`, `schemaVersion` stays 1) holds the waivers only the held-out partition carries, under waiver IDs `contract.json` does not use, and they join the held-out and both views only.
  The development view stays the folder's own `contract.json` bytes and never holds a plan waiver; a plan with no `waivers` leaves every view byte for byte as it was.
  The engine compiles each view with the waivers that view holds, so a waiver it finds incomplete fails the partitions that hold it and leaves the others compiling.
  `check` names a waiver that no view can reach by its waiver ID (a `contract.json` waiver whose condition reads a step the development view lacks, a plan waiver whose condition reads a development-only step or a step no view holds), and a plan waiver ID that collides with `contract.json`'s or repeats.
  `check` keeps the plan's text out of every finding: a waiver is named by its ID only when the ID has the shape `W-nnn` and by its position in `waivers` otherwise, a step a plan waiver reads is named only when it is development-only, and a plan waiver's rule, rationale, condition and approval never reach a finding.
  Removing the held-out view's waiver filter fails the pure view case, the held-out run's isolation scan and the held-out preflight over an incomplete development-only waiver.
  The `corpus.md` guide teaches the rule with a tagged `held-out-waivers` example that `test:evaluate-guidance` validates through the plan schema, `check` and the engine's contract schema, and the `tea-evaluate` reference and the AD-22 text record it.
  `test:evaluate-partition-plans` holds each view's waivers, the unreachable-waiver findings and the per-view compile.
- The routing, test-review and trace contracts report whole-body coverage (Story 1.100, eval-quality's AD-20 rule 2 and AD-31).
  `whole-body` is satisfied when one oracle's direction and check both address every required response key of an operation at one step, and the baseline listed it for these four contracts.
  `tools/generate-contracts.js` appends one oracle per plan step after each contract's existing oracles, so every earlier id and behavior id holds: `O-051` to `O-061` for `tea-routing-intents`, `O-028` to `O-035` for `tea-routing-controls`, `O-014` for `test-review` and `O-027` and `O-028` for `trace`.
  Each names every required key pointer in its direction and its check: `action` and `reason` of a routing answer, the 23 keys of a verdict and the 22 keys of a trace summary.
  The test-review and trace checks are a `shape` over the object with one `existence` per required key.
  The routing check is a `shape`, a `set-membership` of `action` in the actions the runner's parser accepts and a non-blank `reason`, which fails the reply the parser normalizes to a null `reason`.
  The requirement links name the source of each declaration: `tea-cli-contract/routing-answer-whole-body`, `tea-cli-contract/verdict-whole-body` and the trace workflow's step-05.
  Step-05's `recommendations` key now falls back to an empty array like `rejected_evidence`, so a missing field cannot drop a required key from the summary.
  The generator's read of step-05's summary literal refuses a line it cannot place as a lowercase `key: value`, and a value that reads the coverage matrix (by dot or bracket) or an optional chain with no fallback, falls back to `undefined` or is empty; it follows a bare identifier to its single-line declaration.
  A declaration across several lines or a value built by a call is out of that read's scope, and the real runs of the trace workflow and `test:cli` are the guard there.
  `test:contract-oracles` freezes the type of each required and each conditional key beside its name, and requires each README table row to have as many cells as its header.
  No required key is narrowed, because the runner or workflow always emits each of them, and `test/contracts/README.md` records the evidence for each contract in a table that `test:contract-oracles` holds to the frozen key lists, so a narrowing at the source fails until its row says so.
  `test:test-review-cli` now asserts that each real verdict the CLI writes in its payload runs carries every key `VERDICT_KEYS` declares always.
  `test:contract-oracles` compares each oracle with a scorer twin on stored and planted evidence, and scores the zero-action probe through eval-quality against each contract, against the contract without the oracles, against oracles that read one key, every key but one, or name one key fewer in the direction, and against the one-key oracle widened again.
  A direction that names a key the check does not read is refused at compile, which the suite asserts for every key.
  The probe records derive each new oracle's disposition from its scorer twin, and `test:probe-corpus` holds that to a summary or verdict that loses a key and to a routing answer with no reason.
  `test/probes/expected-strength.json` drops `whole-body` from the four suites and `success-indicator-separation` from the two routing suites, since the new routing oracle reads the success indicator beside a payload key.
  Every probe verdict and exit code, and every probe file, is unchanged.
  No eval-quality change and no release.
- The trace, nfr, test-design and ci probe records derive each oracle's disposition from its paired scorer over the stored run it carries (Story 1.94, CAP-12). Their builders in `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` fixed every disposition at `held`, so a `CI_CORRECT_RUNS` row pointing at another project's workflow, or a replay case that was not the correct run of its set, passed `test:probe-corpus`. Each oracle now reads the run of its own project or set through the scorer `tools/generate-contracts.js` pairs with it (`workflowHoldsToken` for ci, the harness's `scoreRun` for trace, nfr and test-design), and `test:probe-corpus` fails with the oracle that no longer holds. The baseline recorded no oracle disposition: the clean control passes pre-flight and scores `CONCERNS` with exit 0, and `probeSummary` keeps only the `probeId`, `state`, `severity` and `trialIndex` of each outcome, so a moved disposition never reached `expected-strength.json`. Only rows that change the run a defect probe carries moved it anyway (six of the 11 caught deviation rows and the rows between the full and minimal projects; none of the evaluation-plan project's, which only the clean control reads). A second check reads every set through another set's run, reads each leg through every other stored case of its suite, reads a run the harness refuses to score through every leg, and requires every oracle to fail under a scorer in at least one wrong read, so a constant `held` on any set or oracle fails, as does a builder that stops exposing what the checks read; a violation a refusal produced counts only for the `run-measured` oracles. The three ci `run-measured` oracles, whose scorer reads no workflow, are listed with the reason, and the two test-design projection-coherence oracles hold with the projection intact and fail only through a projection with its `design` key dropped. The test-review record now derives its verdict-payload and exit-code oracles from the stored verdict, and `test:probe-corpus` reads that verdict with one field dropped at a time, on the first and on the last finding. A `CI_CORRECT_RUNS` row pointed at a stored deviation fails for 11 of the 34 constructed ci deviations; the other 23 pass through the substring oracles and are listed in `test/probes/README.md`. `test/probes/expected-strength.json` is unchanged. Two ci oracles do not hold on the real capture of the evaluation-plan project, which quotes its folder names for the shell (`command-evaluation-install` and `command-evaluation-ci-pr`).
  Story 1.122 makes both hold, and `KNOWN_UNHELD` is empty.
- Every controlled-mutation probe of the test-review, trace, nfr and ci corpora now earns `rollbackVerified: true` from a performed cycle (Story 1.99, CAP-7, AD-8). `tools/generate-probes.js` wrote the claim as a constant for these eighteen probes, each citing two stored files, with the nine test-review probes naming a seeded spec file and the other nine naming a corpus's `ground-truth.json` as the artifact they mutate. The generator now runs each through `runMutationCycle` in a disposable copy under the runtime's private root: the clean arm scores a stored correct artifact, one exact `replace-exact` edit is applied, the mutated arm scores it, the original bytes are restored and their digest compared, and the clean arm scores again. The arm is the probe's own contract oracle, resolved by eval-quality over the artifact the workspace holds. The generator emits no probe when a step fails or when the cycle's digests are not those of the stored bytes it cites, and it holds no `rollbackVerified: true` literal. `test:evaluate-boundaries` scans every file under `tools/` and `test/` that requires the cycle and the generator's whole relative-require closure (derived, so a builder moved to a new file, or into a helper the generator requires, is scanned) and reports any value written to `rollbackVerified` that a cycle's result did not hand over, as a property, an assignment, a default, a binding or a class field. An identifier is read through its declarations, defaults and assignments in the file (a defaulted parameter, a destructuring default, a name taken out of a literal array or object, an alias of an alias), a member expression may not read a literal array or object, directly, through aliases or through `obj.key = value` assignments, and `??` and `?:` pass when every branch is handed over or `false`, so `!0`, `1`, `[!0][0]`, an alias bound to one of them and a comparison fail it as `true` does. A member read of a call over a literal (`Object.values({ ok: !0 })[0]`) takes a type system to resolve and is the one form the walker hands over. The derivation of the scanned files takes the root, and a planted tree with a generator, a helper and a sub-helper holds the closure and its union with the files that require the cycle.
  Each corpus states its mutation. Test-review withholds one row's finding from the stored review that reports every plant. Trace withholds one coverage gap from the seeded set's correct summary. Nfr withholds one domain's finding from the gapped bundle's correct audit (an invented threshold, a missed reliability breach, an omitted section). Ci edits the correct pipeline of its project into a twin: the weekly schedule trigger removed, the `contents: read` block removed, or the template's burn-in job appended. The eighteen twins are under `test/fixtures/probe-mutants/`, each the stored reference with its one named edit and nothing else (the test-review twins keep the reference's `$comment` and ledger fields and are the reference with that row's finding lines removed, the nfr performance twin writes an invented target on the four threshold lines that read UNKNOWN, the maintainability twin is the audit with that section deleted, and the trace twins move the criterion's band, the inventory and the gate criteria that read it, with the P0 twin also moving the gate decision and dropping the recommendation that named the gap). The derived `replace-exact` operator may not span the whole reference, since any two files differ by that edit. The suites vet the twins the builder's emitted probes cite, read off each probe, and assert the set of oracles that flip between reference and twin, each from held to violated.
  The regenerated probes name the artifact each cycle mutated (`targetArtifact`, `mutationSource`, `baselinePassEvidence`, `mutatedFailEvidence`, `artifactDigest`). The AC-8 and AC-10 trace probes and the nfr maintainability probe now name the oracle that can see their mutation, since withholding AC-8, AC-10 or maintainability leaves the gate and the overall status at FAIL; the AC-2 probe names the gate oracle, since its twin carries the PASS the P0 band at 100% derives. The nfr maintainability witness used to read the overall status, which holds on the reference and on the twin alike; it now reads the section's first criterion at CONCERNS, which the gapped audit carries and the clean bundle's audit does not.
  The cycle's direction is the same for the four corpora, and the probes now say so.
  The plant lives in the system's input (a spec file, a seeded set, a gapped bundle, the request in a project's docs), so the manifestation witness fires on the correct run the cycle's clean arm scores and is silent on the mutated artifact, which models a run that misses the plant.
  Pre-flight needs that direction: its fault leg replays the correct run on the planted input, and the witness has to fire there and stay silent on the clean legs.
  Each rationale states the direction.
  The kit resolves every committed probe's witness over both stored artifacts with the shipped arm and holds each corpus to `plant-reported`.
  Every probe verdict and exit code in `test/probes/expected-strength.json` is unchanged, and the corpus digests of the four corpora and three behavior ids moved with the probes.
  `test:test-review-qualification`, `test:trace-qualification`, `test:nfr-qualification` and `test:ci-qualification` join the `npm test` chain (chain length 115, 0.8 seconds each in the shard weights) and plant each failing step per corpus. Through the corpus's builder, six steps are planted in the cycle itself (a clean arm that fails, a mutated arm that holds, a restore that cannot be written, a restored digest that differs, a clean rerun that fails, and a stored reference or mutated artifact that changes while the cycle runs, over a scratch copy of the file) and each emits no probe; five forged cycle results (a claim with no verified rollback, a pre-mutation, restored or mutated digest that is not the stored one, a last re-pass that did not hold, a claim with no digests) are rejected the same way. Against the cycle directly, the kit also plants a restore that fails, a digest that differs, a rerun over the mutated bytes, an inconclusive mutated arm and a stored artifact that is not UTF-8. The cycle hands every arm the workspace file alone (the test-design arm included), so each phase scores the bytes that phase holds and no text reaches it from outside the workspace; the kit's tamper cases run the shipped arm on that input whole and assert its keys are exactly `phase` and `file`. The kit checks that every workspace and its private parent lie outside the checkout and are gone whatever depth the corpus's artifact sits at, and that `git status` of the checkout, ignored files included (`node_modules`, `.claude/`, `_bmad/`, whose `render/` directories agent sessions write while a suite runs, and `.DS_Store` aside; `_bmad-output/` is watched), is what it was, so a cycle that leaks a `*.log` or a `coverage/` file shows, and a case over a scratch repository holds that guard to reading ignored paths (dropping `--ignored` fails a suite). The shared cycle moved to `test/lib/mutation-qualification.js`, which `test:test-design-qualification` still holds.
- Evaluate's evaluation of its own skill closes the coverage gaps Story 1.16's run named (Story 1.46, CAP-3, CAP-10, AD-15). `test/evaluations/bmad-testarch-evaluate/` scored `CONCERNS` in every evidence artifact: `success-indicator-separation`, `malformed-input`, `per-record` and `omission-and-completeness` were unsatisfied at `critical`, and B-002 had no seeded probe. The contract now nominates `/status` as its success indicator and reads it beside every answer field, binds the `type-violating` matcher on a declared `stdin.exit` key, declares `/exits` as a collection location and reconciles it against a reference set holding the thirteen rows of the AD-10 exit table. Two behaviors join B-001 and B-002: B-003, Stage 11 lists every exit its table holds, and B-004, Stage 11 refuses a request with no usable exit and gives it no class. Five seeded probes, each with its own defect ID and mutation, join the clean controls; B-002 is seeded because its rule is now stated once. A clean live run through the Claude Code CLI scored `PASS` in all nine artifacts, every clean control `passed-clean-control` and every seeded probe, held-out included, `caught` in five of five trials, and `eval-quality score` reproduced each artifact byte for byte (recorded in `_bmad-output/implementation-artifacts/evaluate/epic-1-proof.md`).
  `references/gaps.md` gains one sentence: a request that names no command or no exit, or an exit the table does not list for that source, has no class, so say so, ask for the source, exit and stderr, and never guess one. `references/inspection.md` states the web-application rule in one sentence, `references/adapters.md` and `references/corpus.md` no longer restate it, and `test:evaluate-guidance` fails on a second statement and on a mutation whose text no longer occurs once in its target. The skill's `assets/evaluation-folder.gitignore` ignores `compiled-contract.json` and `sealed-brief.json`, which Stage 6 writes into the evaluation folder.
  The new `test:evaluate-dogfood` replays the committed folder offline through `tea-evaluate` over real eval-quality, with a deterministic reader of the skill's guides in the model's place. It holds `PASS` with no unsatisfied rule, `passed-clean-control` and `caught` at five trials, the reference set equal to the exit table, and the return of each engine rule when its repair is removed. The evaluation declares `"confinement": false`, since a confined target's private home holds no Claude login. Stories 1.112 to 1.115 file the findings the pull request does not close.
- Every test Story 1.42's review round added for a surviving mutant now fails on that mutant (Story 1.103, AD-23). Thirty-eight mutants, each the smallest edit that collapses a pair lookup back to an operation-ID-only lookup (or drops one half of the pair, or the check beside it), ran in a disposable copy against the suites that read the site. Thirteen survived the review round's tests and the first version of this story's, and each now has a test that fails on it: `score.js` snapshot completeness, undeclared-pair and unclassified-observation lookups, `arm.js` refusing a step whose operation another interface declares or its own interface declares twice, `check.js` principal mappings, gameability registry entries, skill-runner option sets and option-set step matches (including one interface with two operations under two executables), `degenerateAnswer` over one interface with two operations, and `calibrationStepPair`. The ambiguous branch of `operationsByPair` is reachable (an uncited step on a pair declared twice still compiles), so a case now holds it. `check.js` `infrastructureObservation` stays untested because no evidence channel of the engine's pointer grammar reads an observation's `interfaceId`.
  The Story 2.4 capture-record guard (`test:evaluate-ci`, case `the capture-record guard`) now names each failure as a case: a `migrations` entry that carries a `from` or `to` digest, a retyped `wrote` digest, an absent or empty `migrations` list, an `evaluation.json` edited beyond the declared migration (tiers, phases or one appended byte), a migration over an unmigrated file, a migration of another file and a migration declared twice. The guard also requires the migrated file to be the runtime's own serialization, so a whitespace edit no longer passes through the rebuild. Phases re-keyed under an interface the file's registry never declared fail it too, and so does a phase pair the contract beside it does not declare, such as an operation moved to another declared interface.
  Another suite's reaper removed the private parents the interrupted-replay case planted and waited on, so concurrent runs failed about half the time. `test/lib/scratch-directories.js` now keeps a hold beside the private root, keyed by the owner's process id and written before the parent exists, and its reaper leaves a held parent while the holder runs. The holds directory gets the private root's ownership and link checks, a hold is read only as a regular file, and stale holds and staged files are swept. Every case of `test:evaluate-ci`, `test:evaluate-mutation` and `test:test-design-qualification` that plants a parent under a dead pid or reads the parent of a child it kills holds it.
- `test:evaluate-evaluators` proves that `tea-evaluate ci --tier pr` replays an accepted sealed-brief baseline on a runner that lacks the agent CLI (Story 1.78, CAP-13, AD-21). The case replaces the agent command with a tripwire that records any invocation, runs the six `pr` checks of the plan over a copy of the repository, and asserts each row passes (the replay and the gameability row with only eval-quality's CONCERNS as warnings), the replayed evidence artifacts equal the baseline's byte for byte and the wire stays empty. A version read added to the check, replay or oracle-agreement path now fails it; before, only `score` was held to that.
- `test:evaluate-evaluators` holds the gameability check's scoring branch to starting no agent version probe over a sealed-brief baseline (Story 1.79, CAP-13, AD-21).
  Story 1.78's case now runs over a baseline that holds a gameability probe, P-004, so `gameabilityCheck` takes `replayScore` instead of its no-probe return.
  Before the replay the case asserts the accepted baseline holds `probes/P-004.probe.json` with the `gameability` route, a `gameability:P-004` trial set and an evidence artifact whose three trial votes are `caught`.
  After it the case asserts the `gameability` row exits 0 as a pass, carries no `no gameability probe` note, warns with the one CONCERNS line for P-004, prints `P-004: gameability arm scored through eval-quality score, exit 0`, reproduces P-004's evidence byte for byte, and the tripwire log stays empty.
  A version read added at the head of the scoring branch, after `replayScore` or in the scored-arm line of the result loop now fails the case; before, all three passed.
  The unscored-arm branch of the result loop runs only when `score` fails, and `test:evaluate-ci`'s gameability case holds its exit 12.
  The sealed-brief stub agent takes `--quote-observed-verdict`, which makes a failing `verdict-accepted` row quote the `verdict:` line the call's stdout holds.
  A gameability arm's degenerate response prints `verdict: pending`, so the quotation is witnessed there.
  Without the flag the stub quotes `verdict: rejected`, which that stdout lacks, and `score` refuses the arm with exit 3 (unwitnessed quotation), so no baseline can be accepted.
- A confined Linux target reaches the hosts its registry entry authorizes and no other (Story 1.83, AD-8).
  A command, tool-server or HTTP entry that starts a process takes `egress`, a list of `{ "host", "port", "addresses" }` items, for a target that calls a model provider or an outside HTTPS service.
  Every Bubblewrap call keeps a network namespace of its own with a loopback and nothing else, and a call of an entry that lists hosts gets one route out, a proxy the runtime owns.
  The runtime serves an HTTP `CONNECT` proxy on a Unix socket in a private directory of the call, the call's status shim listens on a loopback port of the namespace and connects each connection to that socket, and the target starts with `HTTPS_PROXY`, `https_proxy` and `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1`.
  eval-quality's `evaluateTarget` decides each request as it decides the evaluation's HTTP port's: a host and port no item lists is refused before the name is resolved, the connection goes to a resolved address an item names, the next when one cannot be reached, and to no other, and a refusal answers `403` naming the reason, the host and the entry.
  An entry that lists no host has no proxy and reaches none, and neither does a client that ignores `HTTPS_PROXY`; the host's abstract Unix sockets stay out of reach for every entry.
  The shim announces the proxy in `HTTPS_PROXY` alone and the proxy reads `CONNECT` alone, so a client that opens no `CONNECT` tunnel (a plain `http://` request, a database driver) has no route, and a target that needs one sets `"confinement": false` with a recorded reason.
  A tunnel to a listed host and port carries whatever bytes the client sends, TLS or not.
  A host holds letters, digits, `.`, `-` and `_` and at most 253 bytes, or is an IPv6 address; the proxy answers any other host `400`, `check` refuses an item the proxy could never match (a wildcard such as `*.example.com` among them), and each refusal's detail in `run.json` is cut to 500 characters, so a target cannot grow the record.
  The shim keeps the egress listener off the port a started server was told to bind in the same namespace, which closed a rare `EADDRINUSE` at the server's start.
  The proxy, its socket and its authorization are private to the call: the authorization stays in the runtime's memory, the directory sits beneath the run's private parent, and the end of the call, a failure, a signal that ends the run and the recovery of a killed run's private parent remove it.
  `run.json` records `egress` (each entry that lists hosts with its `host:port` items, in place of `hostNetwork`) and `egressRefusals` (each trial whose proxy refused a request, with the host, the port, the entry, the reason and a count), and the run's summary names those trials.
  `check` parses each item through eval-quality's `parseProbeTargetPolicy` and refuses a host spelled otherwise than a URL spells it, a host and port listed twice and an HTTP entry that names no server; entries that start one target must list the same `egress`.
  An entry that still declares the removed `network` field gets the one finding that names it, for a command, tool-server and HTTP entry alike.
  The reference's `### File-system confinement`, the harness, adapters, run and gaps guides of the Evaluate skill and the `tea-skill-runner` section teach the authorization, and the starter `evaluation.json` lists a model provider for its `tea-skill-runner` entry.
  Story 1.84 teaches it in the CI guide, which needs its two live capture sessions rerun.
  Story 1.131 files the call's temp, port and bridge directories, which a run killed outright still leaves in the temp directory.
- A Bubblewrap target has no route to the host's path-based Unix sockets (Story 1.82, AD-8). A socket file stays reachable through the read-only view of `/`, since a `connect()` needs no write access, so a confined process could connect to `/var/run/docker.sock`, the system bus at `/run/dbus/system_bus_socket` or an agent socket under `/tmp` and ask the host service behind it to run a job outside the sandbox. Each call now lists the host's sockets and mounts an empty device file over each socket file outside the call's own grants, by its real path, so a connection answers `ECONNREFUSED` for every Bubblewrap target whatever its entry's `network`. The list is the kernel's table of bound Unix sockets (`/proc/net/unix`), the socket files beside a listed path that was moved after it bound (OpenSSH's control master does this) and the socket files in `/run`, `/var/run`, `/tmp` and `/var/tmp` and their directories, which finds the Docker socket a container job shares from the host. A socket in the workspace or in a private directory of the call, the bridge's directory included, stays connectable, and a started HTTP service stays reachable through the bridge. A path goes into the list only after `lstat` on its exact path finds a socket file, so a file name that is no UTF-8, a directory the runtime cannot search or a file another user removed cannot stop a call, and a call hides at most as many sockets as its own command leaves room for, and at most 2,000 in any case, since Bubblewrap counts the target's arguments and the mounts against one bound of 9,000, and the mounts reach Bubblewrap through a file (`--args`). The list is ranked by who can create a socket before it is cut: the Docker, containerd, Podman, system bus and systemd sockets by name, then the sockets of root and of the system accounts, then those of the user running the call, then every other user's in turns (the first socket of each owner, then the second of each), so a local user who makes sockets in bulk cannot push out another user's socket while the room left after those holds one socket for each owner, and `/dev` and `/proc`, which the vector replaces, take no room. The socket files beside a moved path join the list in the same order of owners (the directories are read by who owns them and each socket is charged to its own owner, so one user's directory of 200,000 socket files costs a bounded list, never overflows the stack and cannot push out the moved socket of root, of a system account, of the user running the call or of a third user, whichever directory the kernel's table names first); the one bound left is the socket files of one other user beside a moved path past the room, which stay reachable and are counted. A call is refused (exit 12) when the sockets only root, the system accounts and the user running the call can create exceed the room, which no other user can cause, and `run.json`'s `hostSocketTruncation` and the run's summary name each trial whose calls left sockets of other users reachable because the room ran out. The launcher that hands Bubblewrap the file leaves the target's environment as the call gave it for every variable whose name is a valid shell identifier and that the shell does not initialize, and restores `PWD`, `SHLVL`, `OLDPWD`, `_`, `IFS`, `OPTIND` and `PPID`; a name no shell can hold (`my.setting`, `BASH_FUNC_f%%`), an exported shell function and the variables bash initializes itself (`PS1`, `PS2`, `PS4`, `LINENO`, `RANDOM`, `SHELLOPTS`, `BASHOPTS`, `BASH`, `BASH_VERSION`) can still reach the target changed or missing when a call hides sockets, which the reference states and Story 1.89 closes. A call whose Bubblewrap cannot start because a hidden socket went away is started again over its own list without the vanished sockets, so churn on a busy host cannot keep a call from starting; a tool server or a command whose shim ran is never started again. A socket a host process binds after a call started, one bound in another network namespace outside those directories, one whose file name is no UTF-8, one of another user's past the room, and a second path to the same socket file through a hard link or another mount stay reachable for that call, and the audit lists a connection to any of them as an observed mount (Story 1.86). Story 1.87 gives a macOS target a rule on the socket's path, Story 1.88 hides the sockets from the evaluation layer's processes, and Story 1.89 gives the target's exact environment through the launcher. `host-sockets.js` lists the sockets, the isolation golden gains the vector of a call that hides some, `the host socket record` drives a cut report from the registry's port to `run.json` and the summary, and `the path socket route` runs in the ubuntu job. The run guide and the gaps guide tell an adopter that a Linux target reaches no host service through a socket file under either `network` value (Docker for testcontainers, a database's Unix socket) and opts out with `"confinement": false` and a recorded reason, and the run guide names `hostSocketTruncation`.
- `evaluation.json` can declare a `partitionPlan` that keeps interaction plan steps inside the partition that owns them (Story 1.51, AD-9, AD-22). `run --partition` used to filter only the scored probes while every arm executed the whole `contract.json` plan, so a development run launched and recorded a request meant for held-out probes. Development-only steps stay in `contract.json`, named by `partitionPlan.developmentOnlySteps`; held-out steps and their oracles live in a sealed plan under `corpus/held-out/` (`held-out-plan.schema.json`). One pure view, `contractView`, derives each partition's contract at the single choke point of the preflight pipeline, so launches, the run's `contract.json`, `eval-contract.json`, the sealed brief, observations, trial records and a baseline copied from the run carry only that partition's steps. The development view is the folder's own bytes and never opens the plan; with no `partitionPlan` every view is the source bytes, so no committed baseline or replay changes. A step is read from a `pointer`, a `captured` binding, `evidenceTargets`, a rubric criterion's `evidence`, a waiver's `condition` or an `after` clause only, so a literal, commentary or scope that spells a pointer neither drops a shared oracle from the held-out view nor reaches a `check` finding. `check` names a held-out step, oracle or behavior by ID only when the ID has the schema's shape and otherwise by its index, prints schema paths with every adopter-chosen key replaced by `*`, and refuses a held-out plan path that is not a regular file directly under the folder's own `corpus/held-out/`. A development `preflight` or `run` that falls back on an unparsable probe file stays a development run. A behavior declared with no oracle is legal under a partition plan.
  `preflight` gains `--partition development|held-out`, qualifying only that partition's probes over its view (an unknown partition exits 64, an empty held-out set exits 10). `check` adds the `partition-plan` rule: a development-only step the contract lacks, a plan that is absent, unparsable or off its schema, an ID collision, a dangling `after` or pointer, a behavior left with no oracle in the held-out view, a held-out probe's behavior with more than one, a non-deterministic evaluator, a gameability probe, and a rubric or waiver that reads a development-only step (Story 1.105 gives a rubric criterion a home by the steps it reads and refuses only one no view reaches). Findings name paths and IDs and never quote the plan. `ci` compiles the baseline's own partition view when it compares the contract digest, so a held-out or both baseline replays with no stale warning.
  Story 1.105 partitions rubrics. Waivers, evaluator mappings, gameability probes, compile and seal of each view in `ci`, and one designated oracle per behavior in the both view are not partitioned yet and are filed as Stories 1.106 to 1.110. Story 1.111 files the `SKILL.md` Stage 6 `--partition development` clause, which waits on the live capture recapture, and a refusal of a `preflight` with no `--partition` under a `partitionPlan`; until then `references/adapters.md` and `references/run.md` carry the correction, and the guides name the held-out baseline under `baseline/` as closed to the authoring loop beside `corpus/held-out/`. The `partition-plan` fixture and `test:evaluate-partition-plans` hold the isolation checks; the corpus guide teaches the plan with two tagged, validated examples, and the AD-9 and AD-22 amendments and the Story 1.51 criteria record the shape.
- `tea-evaluate run` records how much of the macOS audit the kernel's log lost (Story 1.81, AD-7, AD-8).
