The working form runs each harness twice, once with `--json` and once without, and requires each fixture's read count to be exactly one higher in the run that reaches the write. Reverting `digestFiles`/`writeRecord` in `test/lib/eval-record.js` was watched to fail all six cases, including the pre-existing review-harness one, and restoring them was watched to pass all six.
  `compileValidator`'s schema read in `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` gets the same discipline: one scripted case against the isolation-manifest schema, driven through real scoring in `test/test-probe-corpus.js`, because `validateArtifact` is reached only that way. `CORPUS_HARNESSES[2]`, a numeric index into a table 300 lines away, is now `corpusHarness('routing')`, a lookup by name. `unreadable()`'s `chmod 0o000` proof does not hold under root, which does not enforce permissions against its own process, and the one assertion that depended on it used to report a vacuous pass there. `skipIfRoot()` now prints a loud, distinct skip line for that assertion instead, verified against a run with `process.getuid` mocked to 0.
- `npm run test:schema-versions`, which holds every `schemaVersion` TEA writes to the constant the installed `eval-quality` exports for that kind. It runs in the chain after the package's own corpus check and before the generator diffs, so a bump is named before `--check` reports it as changed bytes. It reads each constant from `require('eval-quality')` itself and holds `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` to that reading first, then the three builders in `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js`, the two generator constants, every probe in the fourteen committed corpora, all fourteen contracts and the scoring policy, each failure naming the artifact kind, the stamp found and the stamp the package reads, in the words the package's own `schema-version-mismatch` fault uses. A source scan over `test/lib/` and `tools/` refuses a literal integer stamp, because a revert to a literal that agrees with the package today passes every value comparison and drifts on the next bump. The published `artifact-reference` schema is read to confirm it still declares no stamp, so TEA's recorded exemption cannot outlive its reason. The helpers' other paths run in the same check: a disagreeing stamp reports both numbers, an absent one reports its absence, `artifact-reference` throws with the reason it carries no stamp, and a kind TEA does not write throws naming it. An absent package exits 2 with nothing measured; there is no skip.
  Proved by moving `PROBE_SCHEMA_VERSION` from 5 to 6 in the installed `dist` and running the check: exit 1, with all 55 probes across the fourteen corpora reported as `probe carries "schemaVersion" 5 where this build reads 6`, each under its file and probe id, and the builders and generators reported as agreeing because they read the moved constant. Moving `EVAL_CONTRACT_SCHEMA_VERSION` names all fourteen contracts the same way, and moving `SCORING_POLICY_SCHEMA_VERSION` names `test/probes/scoring-policy.json`. Reverting `tools/generate-probes.js` to a literal `4` fails naming that file with both numbers, and reverting `sealedRunRecord` to a literal `6`, which agrees with the package, passes every value comparison and fails the source scan at the line.
- The `bmad-testarch-nfr` gate artifact declares the four domain statuses. `nfr-report-template.md`'s Gate YAML snippet carried `overall_status` and the eight ADR Quality Readiness Checklist categories and nothing for the four domains Step 4 dispatches a worker for, while `resources/nfr-assessment.example.md` had been publishing an `audited_domains` block the template never declared and `checklist.md` had been asking for a domain status the snippet had nowhere to put. The template now carries that block, `nfr-status-definitions.md` states the rule that produces each value under a new "Domain Status" section, step-04E computes the four statuses by that rule and step-05 writes them.
  `test/eval-nfr.js` reads the declared block, and reads the `## <Domain> Assessment` section beside it: the two are one judgment written twice, so `maxGateDisagreements` is zero and a report that answers differently in the two places fails. A domain counts toward `domainCoverage` only when the block declares it and the document assesses it, so neither half alone is the deliverable. `test/fixtures/nfr-eval/ground-truth.json` rests `domainStatusIsWorstFinding` on the stated rule; it had rested on step-04E's compliance rollup by analogy, because the workflow stated no rule for a domain's own status.
  Proved by three measurements. The new stored case `gapped-domain-statuses-only-in-prose` is the correct gapped audit in the artifact shape the workflow published before this change: it records `coverage.present` 0 of 4 and four domain statuses reported as nothing, where the same audit carrying the block records 4 of 4 and four hits, and the contract's new `domain-block` oracle resolves false on it. The new stored case `gapped-gate-contradicts-sections` publishes `security: 'CONCERNS'` over a section that rolls up PASS and records the one gate disagreement the ceiling of zero admits none of. And replacing the rule sentence in `nfr-status-definitions.md` fails `npm run test:eval-nfr-data` with "section \"Domain Status\" no longer contains ... so the rule this corpus rests on is gone"; restored, it passes.
  Five stored cases are new in all, the other three holding the readings a corpus of correct blocks cannot see: a gate declaring a domain the document never assesses, a gate block above headings no domain section recognises, and a block that appears only inside a quoted worked example. Every one of the fourteen stored results that scored before is byte-identical apart from the three new fields, so a score before and after is comparable on the same input. `SCORER_VERSION` moved to 9, the nfr contract states ten oracles where it stated eight, and `test/probes/expected-strength.json` moved only its corpus digest and its behavior ids; no probe score moved.
  A domain the gate block names twice now counts as the artifact contradicting itself whether or not the two lines agree, holding the gate to the same rule `duplicateDomainSections` already holds a repeated section to. `custom_nfr_categories` is not named in `audited_domains`: no worker in Step 4 dispatches for a custom category and no findings list exists to roll one up, so its status stays in its own report section rather than a block the workflow has no way to fill for it.
  Proved by re-running the corpus with `security` declared twice at one status: `gateDisagreements` records one entry where it recorded none before. `npm run test:eval-nfr-data`, `npm run test:contract-oracles` and `node test/test-eval-replay.js` all pass with the fix in place.
- `npm run test:direction`, which runs the `dependency-direction` gate `eval-quality` publishes over `cli/`, `tools/`, `test/` and `src/**/*.cjs`, held to the layer graph in `eval-quality.config.json`: `cli/` may import only itself, `tools/` and `test/` may import each other and `cli/`, and the one `.cjs` hook under `src/` may import only itself. A gate over all of `src/` would be vacuous, since 723 of its 724 files carry no code at all. `reportOnly` is now `false`: the gate fails the build on a violation rather than only reporting one.
  The first run reported 6 violations, all since fixed: `cli/lib/review-provenance.js`'s relative `require('../../package.json')` read `fs` instead, since a require escaping every declared root is unplaceable; `tools/validate-eval-schemas.js`'s computed `require(path.join(...))` over the eval suite manifest's known harness paths became a literal lookup table (six at the time, later kept current as `ci` and `atdd` joined the manifest); the layer graph itself was corrected so `test/` may import the hook it tests; two dynamic imports in `test/test-contract-oracles.js` that reached `eval-quality`'s unexported `dist/core` internals for `resolveCheck` and its evidence-resolution helpers now read them off the package's public entry point, published in eval-quality 3.3.0; and `test/test-test-review-cli.js:214`'s `require(name) {` method shorthand, a false positive where the gate used to read an object method named `require` as a call site, stopped firing once eval-quality 3.3.0 shipped the fix.
  Rebasing this branch onto a moving `main` surfaced four more violations the first run never saw, each from a file `dependency-direction` had not scanned before: `test/lib/doc-count-sources.js`'s `require()` of `../evals/suite-manifest.json` and `../../package.json` now read both through `fs.readFileSync` and `JSON.parse`, the same fix already applied to `review-provenance.js`; `test/test-schema-versions.js`'s computed `require(path.join(PROJECT_ROOT, file))` over the two generator files became a literal lookup table, the same fix already applied to `validate-eval-schemas.js`; `test/test-contracts.js`'s `--package <path>` flag, which let a maintainer point the check at an unreleased local `eval-quality` build via a dynamic `import()` of an arbitrary path, is removed, since the gate's exemption mechanism reaches a static import declaration only and no config change admits a dynamic one with a computed argument - the same local build is now reached by `npm link`-ing it (or installing its packed tarball) so the check's one literal `import('eval-quality')` resolves to it, which needed no code change at all; and `tools/generate-lockfile-age-cache.js`'s `fetchTimeMap`, reached from `eval-quality`'s own `dist/gates/audit-lockfile-age.mjs` (a module its own comment invites reuse of, but one its `package.json`'s `exports` map does not publish, so the reach was the same class of violation as the two in `test-contract-oracles.js`), is now TEA's own ~20-line implementation of the same one-GET-per-package-name registry query, which keeps `.lockfile-age-cache.json`'s output byte-identical since the gate that reads the cache has no opinion on how an entry was produced.
  `npm run test:lockfile-age-cache` holds that independent implementation to the one contract it and `eval-quality`'s own reach into `dist/gates/audit-lockfile-age.mjs` both read: the npm registry's `time` map, confirmed from the installed package's source to be all its `fetchTimeMap` does with the response either. `wrappy@1.0.2`, already locked in `package-lock.json`, is asserted against its real, immutable publish timestamp, fetched live and pinned here since a publish time never changes once set; the check skips rather than fails when the registry is unreachable, so a disconnected run cannot turn a network gap into a false defect. This does not prove the two implementations agree on every future release: it cannot compare against `eval-quality`'s own `fetchTimeMap` without the exact unexported-internals reach this change removes, so a redefinition on `eval-quality`'s side of what "publication time" means, away from the registry's own `time` map, would pass unnoticed here. What it holds is the realistic failure: TEA's own copy regressing, proven by breaking the field read (`meta.time` to `meta.versions`) and watching the check name the wrong value it got instead.
  `npm run test:layering-boundary-lineage` gained two fixtures proving the flip rather than only the manual checks above: `direction-violation`, a `cli/` file reaching into `tools/`, which the gate must refuse at exit 1; and `direction-require-shorthand`, an object literal's `require(name) {` method definition, which the gate must pass at exit 0 so the 3.3.0 fix regresses here rather than being read off a repository that happens to report zero violations today.
- `npm run test:boundary`, which runs the `package-boundary` gate over the published `src/`, `cli/` and `.claude-plugin/` trees, plus `README.md`, `LICENSE` and `package.json`'s `description` and `keywords`, against three patterns: this repository's own dev files under `test/`, a build-machine's absolute path, and a relative reference to an unshipped `docs/how-to`, `docs/reference` or `docs/explanation` page. `src/` and `cli/` carry no extension filter, so `cli/examples/*.yml` and every shipped `*.md` are covered too, not only `.js`. It found and fixed a real leak: `library-integration-mandate.md`'s "Verify the copies" step told every skill's runtime context to run `npm run test:knowledge`, a script no installed copy carries, in the one knowledge fragment shipped identically into all nine skills (the agent's own copy and each of the eight workflows'). A dozen more comments and links carrying the same class of reference were reworded or repointed at the published site rather than naming an unshipped path.
  The `docs-path` pattern's own first shape, `(?<!/)\bdocs/(?:how-to|reference|explanation)/...`, missed every relative link `README.md` actually uses: `./docs/reference/configuration.md` has a `/` immediately before `docs`, same as a bare `docs/...` reference, so the negative lookbehind excluded it along with the absolute `/docs/...` paths it was written to exclude. Eleven such links in `README.md` and two doc-only comments in the trace workflow (`src/workflows/testarch/bmad-testarch-trace/steps-c/step-02-discover-tests.md` and `workflow.yaml`) were passing the gate unnoticed. The pattern now excludes a preceding word character or `/` and admits a leading `./` or `../` run, `(?<![\w/])(?:\.\.?/)*docs/...`, which still leaves the `bmad-teach-me-testing` skill's own absolute `/docs/...` dual-path data unflagged. All thirteen references now point at the published site instead. A fixture proves the fix: a seeded `./docs/reference/...` link in `test/fixtures/layering-boundary-lineage/boundary-violation/` fails the gate, and reverting the pattern to its first shape passes it again.
  `tea-dev-test-file` carried the matching flaw in the other direction: `\btest/...` alone matches inside a package specifier that merely contains the segment, such as `require.resolve('@playwright/test/package.json')` in `cli/atdd-red-check.js`, which rebasing onto the ATDD story surfaced. The character before "test" there is "/", the same transition a real `test/foo.js` reference makes from a backtick or a space, so the pattern could not tell the two apart. It now excludes a preceding `/` too, `(?<!/)\btest/...`, proven by the same fixture: the seeded `@playwright/test/package.json` reference stays unflagged, and reverting the exclusion flags it as a third violation where the fixture declares two.
- `npm run test:lineage`, which runs the `field-ownership` gate over `tools/`, holding the probe and contract `schemaVersion` stamp to exactly `tools/generate-probes.js` and `tools/generate-contracts.js`. Scoped to `tools/` on purpose: `schemaVersion` is a name five of `eval-quality`'s twelve published artifact kinds each carry independently, and `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` legitimately stamps three of the other kinds (`evaluatorConfiguration`, `isolationManifest`, `sealedRunRecord`) that these two generators never touch, so the gate would need as many declared writers as artifact kinds if it scanned `test/` too, which would authorize exactly the cross-kind drift it exists to catch. It generalizes a real, narrower incident: a hardcoded copy of the probe schema version once drifted from what `generate-probes.js` actually stamped and passed for months, caught only when a probe's declared leg count stopped matching what the sink recorded; that stale local copy has since been removed in favor of reading the constant the generator exports.
- `package.json` now declares a `files` allowlist (`src`, `cli`, `.claude-plugin`, `README.md`, `LICENSE`). TEA published to npm with no `files` field at all, so the tarball carried the whole repository verbatim: all 400 files under `test/`, the documentation website's source, `.github/`, `.husky/`, `.vscode/`, editor and lint configuration, everything, 1248 files and 16.9 MB unpacked. The trim drops it to 752 files and 10.9 MB, carrying every one of the seven `bin` entries and every skill's `SKILL.md` and `module.yaml`. Verified past what a file-list diff can show: the packed tarball installs into a scratch project outside this repository, every `bin` command resolves and runs, and every skill directory `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` names is present with its `SKILL.md` intact.
- `typescript` (`^7.0.2`) as an explicit devDependency. TEA declared none before; a copy was only ever present transitively through the docs toolchain, resolved to 5.9.3. The `dependency-direction` and `field-ownership` gates read source through `typescript/unstable/ast`, a subpath that exists only from typescript 7 onward. This repository has no `tsconfig.json` and no `.ts` file of its own, and `website/`'s separate lockfile carries no typescript dependency, so the bump is isolated to the two gates that need it.
- `npm run test:guard-publish`, and `tools/guard-publish.js` wired as `prepublishOnly`. TEA had no publish lifecycle hook, so a laptop holding a valid npm token could run `npm publish` directly, skipping the version bump, the changelog stamp, the test run and the provenance the release workflow provides. The guard refuses unless `GITHUB_ACTIONS`, the running workflow's own name and the repository slug all match `.github/workflows/publish.yaml`'s own environment; holding an npm token grants nothing on its own, proven by a test that sets one without those markers and asserts the refusal. A publish through the authorized workflow succeeding is proven on the next real release rather than asserted here.
- `npm run test:lockfile-age` and `npm run test:licences`, which run the `lockfile-age` and `licences` gates `eval-quality` publishes as `eval-quality-gates` over `package-lock.json` and `website/package-lock.json`, configured by `eval-quality.config.json` at the repository root. The seven-day window and the SPDX allowlist are named there explicitly and nothing in the file is a version literal. The one root-lockfile policy admits CC-BY-4.0 for browser-support data reached only through the lint toolchain, the one tolerance admits sharp's optional LGPL-3.0-or-later platform binaries while `website/package.json` stays private, and zod-to-ts, whose published manifest declares no licence, is read as MIT on the evidence of its registry packument and its repository's LICENSE file. Before the policy, the tolerance and that row, the gate reported 27 root and 26 website entries outside the allowlist.
  `.npmrc` sets `min-release-age=7`, and `website/.npmrc` sets the same floor because npm reads a project `.npmrc` from the local prefix and never inherits the root's: `min-release-age` read `null` from `website/` before the file existed. The floor filters resolution only, which is why the audit stays beside it. It re-reads the committed lockfiles and fails closed on a young entry, on an entry whose publish metadata could not be fetched, and on an entry whose resolved URL is not its own registry tarball. `eval-quality` is excluded from the floor and from the window, because TEA pins it exactly and adopts it on its release day by a human's hand, and `npm run test:supply-chain` holds the two exclusion lists equal.
  `test:lockfile-age` runs in its own `supply-chain` CI job but not in the default `npm test` chain: it is the one gate here that queries the public npm registry once per unique locked package name, and `npm test` runs on every local commit through the pre-commit hook, where a registry stall has nothing to do with the change being committed. `test:licences` reads both lockfiles directly with no network call and stays in the default chain.
  Proved on the live lockfiles the day the gate was adopted: the first run reported eval-quality@3.1.0 and electron-to-chromium@1.5.425 published inside the window in the root lockfile, and the website lockfile clean over 471 entries.
- `npm run test:supply-chain`, which seeds one violation per gate under `test/fixtures/supply-chain/` and watches the installed binary refuse it by name: a GPL-3.0-only entry for licences, and for lockfile-age an entry resolved off the registry beside a name the registry does not carry. It also holds the wiring: the two gate scripts call the binary the package's manifest declares, each gate they name has a section in the configuration covering both lockfiles, a gate invoked with no section refuses at exit 64 rather than passing over nothing, and the floor reads 7 from the root and from `website/`. A gate script that exits 0 having executed nothing is what this check exists to catch. Proved by stripping the floor from `.npmrc`, by pointing `test:licences` at a gate the configuration never opted into, and by relicensing the seeded entry as MIT: each fails the check naming the cause, and restored it passes.
- TEA fills `eval-quality`'s `DiagnosticSink`, which `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` accepted on two signatures, forwarded at two call sites and filled from nowhere, so it was `undefined` on every run and reported nothing. `runSuite` now builds the sink itself rather than taking one, because a parameter every caller declines is adoption of nothing. The diagnostics are partitioned per probe by `runId`, which is the only structured field that tells two probes' pre-flights apart, and each scored entry carries its own.
  What the sink is read for is the leg count, and it is the only channel that carries one. `PreflightVerdict` reports checks, and a check is not a leg: 29 of the 55 probes the stored corpus scores plan a number of legs that differs from the number of checks their verdict reports. `preflightLegs` is now recorded for all 55 in `test/probes/expected-strength.json`. `Diagnostic` carries `runId`, `stage` and a free-text `message`, so the leg count is read off the diagnostic count, one closing line over two lines per leg, and a count that is not odd is reported as a moved emission contract rather than divided into a fraction.
  Proved by removing the sink from the `runPreflight` call and running the gate: every probe reports that the sink received nothing and the baseline moves from `"preflightLegs": 4` to `"preflightLegs": null` on the first probe. `npm run eval:preflight` fills the sink the same way and prints the leg count per probe, which is the arm that spawns paid legs.
- Six seeded faults per contract in `npm run test:contracts`, held against the failure code and the issue locations each one must report. Every contract under `test/contracts/` compiles, so the check's blocked path was reached by nothing, and a compiler that stopped naming the failing field would have read as thirteen passes. The six cover both error classes the compiler declares, all four spellings an issue location can take including a key holding both characters RFC 6901 escapes, the array-index collapse the baseline depends on, and the faults that carry no issue list. They run before `--write`, so regenerating the baseline cannot record a channel that has stopped carrying the reason.
  Proved by stripping `{ cause: parsed.error }` from the `RuntimeFault` the installed compiler throws on a failed parse: 39 seeded faults report an empty `issueShapes` where a location is recorded, and the check exits 1. Restored, it passes.
- `strictPromotable` is recorded for all 55 scored probes in `test/probes/expected-strength.json`, and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md` records the decision it feeds. `eval-quality`'s binary promotes a CONCERNS verdict to exit `1` under `--strict`, except a CONCERNS firing only on AD-21's two evidence conditions, and the ladder publishes that answer itself. TEA scores in process through `runScore`, so the promotion was neither applied nor declined; it was absent.
  TEA declines it, in one constant, `STRICT_CONCERNS_PROMOTION` in `test/lib/probe-scoring.js`, with the reason on the page that fixes TEA's exit classes: exit `1` here already means a measured quality failure decided by baseline movement, and promoting would give it a second meaning inside one repository. All 33 CONCERNS are `strictPromotable` today, every one firing on an unsatisfied coverage gap, so the day one fires only on an evidence condition the baseline moves with the decision unchanged.
  Proved by flipping the constant to `true` and running the gate: 33 baseline entries move from `"exitCode": 0` to `"exitCode": 1`. `ladderExitCode` is the only place a ladder resolution becomes an exit code TEA reports, in the deterministic gate and in the live harness both. The 22 probes that resolve at the Invalid rung carry `strictPromotable: true` from a hardcode and can never move; the 33 CONCERNS are the ones the field speaks for.
- `baselineDifferences` in `test/eval-contract-strength.js` is driven by `npm run test:probe-corpus`, which is the first check ever to run it. It is the function deciding whether a paid live run passed, it lives in a file exposed only through `eval:contract-strength` and `eval:preflight`, and neither is in `npm test`, so it had no gate at all. The deterministic gate already scores every probe, and the rows the comparator reads are a pure function of that score, so driving it needs no credential and no model call. It is run once over the rows that produced the baseline, which must report nothing, and once per field with that field moved, which must report exactly one difference in the right class. Proved by deleting the leg-count comparison and watching the gate fail with "the live comparator reported 0 environment difference(s) for a moved preflightLegs".
  The live harness compares `preflightLegs` on the same terms, in the environment class, beside the pre-flight outcome it already compared. The pre-flight plan is a pure function of the contract and the probes, which both arms share, so a live run whose sink stopped being filled now reports `null` against a recorded integer instead of printing a bare `?` and passing.
- A pre-flight that planned no legs is a reported problem in `npm run test:probe-corpus`. It is the one pre-flight outcome that reduces to `passed` having probed nothing, and every probe in every corpus plans two, three or four.
- `tools/validate-ci-coverage.js` holds the other half of its own claim: every name the `npm test` chain calls has a definition in `scripts`. It held that a chained script runs somewhere in CI and nothing held that the script exists, so a rebase resolution taking one side of the chain string could drop a definition while keeping the entry that calls it. `npm run` reports a missing script only when the chain reaches it, which on a rebase is after the commit, so the failure arrived with nothing naming the cause. The check names the script and exits 1, proved by deleting a definition while leaving its chain entry and watching it fire.
  It exists because the hazard recurred within an hour on the author of its own finding, who caught it the second time only by having just written it down and checking for it deliberately. Knowing to look is not a defence, which is the whole argument for the check.
- `npm run test:file-system-conformance`, which runs `eval-quality`'s published file-system port conformance suite against the shipped `createNodeFileSystemAdapter`. Twelve assertions, six per method: a failing mechanism becomes a declared AD-28 fault with a non-empty artifactPath rather than a bare `Error`, exactly one underlying call on success and one on failure so no hidden retry passes, an aborted signal settles the call rather than leaving it pending, a mechanism that returns an error-shaped value has it thrown rather than handed back, and what resolves parses against the published response schema. Twelve emitted, twelve published, all twelve passing. It lands before any call site moves, which is the point: FR19's conformance clause is satisfied first, so the cutover that follows has a certified baseline rather than a simultaneous claim.
- The count agreeing with the package is not the verdict and the check says so. A suite handed a subject it cannot build still produces the full twelve, and this was measured rather than assumed: bare objects with no `build` give twelve outcomes, a count that agrees with the constant, zero passing, and `subject.build is not a function` as the first detail. So both halves are asserted, the emitted count against `CONFORMANCE_OUTCOME_COUNTS['file-system']` read through `test/lib/conformance-counts.js`, and every outcome passing.
- `npm run test:conflict-markers`, which fails when a tracked text file carries an unresolved merge conflict, and it runs first in the chain. It exists because one shipped: `CHANGELOG.md` reached `main` carrying a whole conflict block, `<<<<<<< HEAD` through `=======` to a trailing marker, past a green CI run, past ESLint, past markdownlint, past Prettier and past review. Nothing in the chain was looking for it. It is easy to assume `lint:md` covers this and the reason it does not is the part worth keeping: a trailing `>>>>>>> <sha>` is not markdown, so Prettier reflows it into `> > > > > > > <sha>`, a seven-deep blockquote, which is valid markdown. Two instances landed within one night. The first sat next to a heading, where the reflowed block changed the heading level and `MD001` fired, so it was caught and it looked as though something was watching. The second sat between two bullets, disturbed nothing and shipped. A gate that catches a class only when the class happens to disturb something else it does look at is not a gate for that class. The check reads `git ls-files`, skips binary content by a NUL check rather than by an extension list, and reports the bare `=======` divider only in a file that also carries an opening or a closing marker, because seven equals signs alone are a markdown setext heading underline. All four shapes were reintroduced and watched to fail with the file and line, and a setext underline was confirmed not to fire.
- TEA reads elapsed time through `eval-quality`'s `createSystemClockAdapter` rather than off the wall clock in each harness. Seven harnesses called `Date.now()` directly at thirty-six sites and each decided for itself what a duration was, so a `durationMs` in a `--json` result record was a number no test could pin: every assertion about one had to be "some non-negative integer", which a stopped clock satisfies. `test/lib/clock.js` owns the port and every harness reads through it.
- `runClockPortConformance` runs in `test/test-probe-conformance.js` beside the command-probe arm. Six outcomes, all passing, and the count is read through `expectedOutcomeCount`, which every arm naming a file now uses so a missing entry fails with the arm named. The `clock` entry in `CONFORMANCE_ARMS` moves from a recorded reason to the file that runs it.
- `npm run test:clock-port` proves the port is in the path that produces a duration, which the conformance arm cannot. The conformance runner builds its own adapter and never touches a TEA call site, so a story that adopted the port, certified it, and left every harness on `Date.now()` would pass that arm unchanged. `TEA_CLOCK_FIXTURE` names a file of ISO instants and the mechanism serves them in order, so a harness run under it reports scripted durations. The fixture scripts whole-hour gaps, which no real stub run can produce, and the check asserts every recorded duration is a whole number of those hours and that none is small enough to have come from the system clock. Measured, with the substitution named so a reader can reproduce the number. Replacing `nowMs`'s body alone with `Date.now()` fails eleven of the twenty assertions; replacing every export, so `nowIso` returns `new Date().toISOString()` too, fails seventeen. The port restored passes all twenty. Reverting one harness alone fails two of them, and putting a single `new Date().toISOString()` back into one record stamp fails two, which is the case a review found the first draft missed: the check pinned a single harness, so five of the thirty-eight call sites were covered and reverting the other thirty-three shipped green.
- The check covers every harness rather than one. It runs each of the five that write a suite-result record under the scripted clock in its cheapest no-model mode, reaches `eval-all.js` through the one branch that writes a full summary and spawns nothing, asserts every duration and the record's `generatedAt` came from the port, and scans all seven harness sources for any reintroduced host-clock read. The scan covers `Date.now`, `performance.now`, `process.hrtime` and a no-argument `new Date()`, because grepping for `Date.now()` alone missed the exact line the `generatedAt` fix removed: putting it back passed the scan while reintroducing the defect. It requires no call parenthesis, because requiring one named `process.hrtime` as refused while refusing only the deprecated tuple spelling and letting `process.hrtime.bigint()` through, which is the documented current form. `new Date(mark)` stays allowed, since an argument makes it a conversion of a mark the port already gave. The scan is the only cover for `eval-contract-strength.js`, which writes a cost report under its own shape and declines `--agent-cmd`.
- `generatedAt` in every result record comes from the port. It was `new Date().toISOString()` inside `test/lib/eval-record.js`, so a scripted run produced a record whose durations were scripted and whose stamp was real, describing a run that began in March and was generated in September while passing its own schema.
- `writeSuiteResult`, `writeRunSummary` and `eval-contract-strength.js`'s own cost-report writer refuse to write while `TEA_CLOCK_FIXTURE` is set, unless the caller opts in. Left set in a shell the variable turns a real eval run scripted in silence, and `test/eval-all.js` spawns its children with the parent environment, so it reaches every one of them. The output would be a committed evidence record carrying fabricated durations that passes its own schema.
- The temporary skill directory `test/test-clock-port.js` creates to reach `eval-all.js`'s no-spawn branch is removed on `SIGINT` and `SIGTERM` as well as in a `finally`, and swept unconditionally at the start of every run. A `finally` covers a throw and covers no signal, and the leftover is a landmine git cannot show: it is an empty directory, so nothing tracks it and `git status` stays clean, while `npm run test:eval-schemas` fails on an unaccounted skill that appears in no diff. Reproduced and confirmed healed.
- `test/eval-all.js` catches a rejected `main`. It was the one asynchronous entry point without one, and an unhandled rejection exits 1 on node, which in this repository is the `quality` class: an infrastructure throw would have reported as a measured quality failure with no record. Its first clock read also moved below argv handling, so `--help` and a usage error cost no reading.
- The scripted base sits decades from wall-clock time, and the distance is load-bearing. A half-reverted run taking its start mark from `Date.now()` and its end mark from the scripted clock produces a delta that distance makes impossible to mistake for a real one, and `elapsedMsSince` clamps a negative delta to zero, so zero is the signature of a half-revert rather than a fast run and both checks reject it.
- The scripted clock is exhaustible on purpose. A run asking for more instants than the fixture declares throws rather than falling back to the system clock, because a silent fallback would let the check pass against the clock it exists to rule out. That behaviour is itself asserted.
- `bmad-testarch-test-design` has a behavioral eval suite, so it is no longer the eighth skill in the manifest's `deferred` array. Fragment selection already measured which knowledge a run loads, which is a routing decision taken before the workflow produces anything; nothing measured the document. A risk register is the easiest artifact in this repository to fake: six categories, a 1-3 scale each way, and a product. A list of nine plausible risks fits any feature ever written, and read on its own it is indistinguishable from an analysis of the feature that was actually supplied. `test/eval-test-design.js` asks four questions of the produced document and a parser answers each one: is every reported risk traceable to a sentence of the epic, is every probability and impact on the workflow's own scale with every score the product of the two, do priorities respect the fixture's severity ordering pairwise, and does every material risk reach the coverage plan at a test level the fixture admits. The corpus under `test/fixtures/test-design-eval/` is two epics, one carrying five material risks and four risks it rules out in as many words, and one clean control whose genuine risk set is small. The clean control is what stops recall from rewarding a run that reports everything: a suite with only a seeded set is passed by a workflow that emits the same nine risks whatever it is handed.
- Priority is scored as an ordering and never as a function of the risk score. The workflow says the opposite of that derivation in three places it owns: `resources/knowledge/test-priorities-matrix.md` states "Priority is **not derived from** risk score", `resources/knowledge/probability-impact.md` repeats it, and the epic-level template's per-priority criteria read "Risk score is supporting evidence and is not a required condition". A suite asserting `score >= 6 implies P0` would fail a workflow that followed its own rule. What is checkable without taking that position is the ordering, so every strictly ordered pair of fixture severity ranks becomes a pairwise constraint and equal ranks constrain nothing. A pair where either risk went unreported, or reached no priority section, is unresolvable and leaves the denominator rather than passing for free, and a run resolving no pair scores the metric unmeasurable, which is a failure.
- A band placement check, which caught a gap in the suite's own first draft. The scorer read each row's `Score`, `Probability` and `Impact` cells and never looked at which heading the row sat under, so a score-9 risk filed under `Low-Priority Risks (Score 1-2)` scored clean. The check reads the declared range out of the heading rather than hardcoding the template's, so a run that bands its register differently is held to what it said. Its pair of answers is pinned by the replay corpus: a 9 under `Score 1-2` fails and the same 9 under `Score >=6` passes.
- `cli/test-design-runner.js`, the `tea-test-design-runner` command the contract names, registered in `test/lib/probe-targets.js` and in `package.json`'s `bin`. It is `tea-trace-runner` with one artifact instead of two: a prompt on standard input, one `runAgent` call at `scoped-artifact-writes`, and the workflow's document left on disk. It reads no artifact, because the caller behind eval-quality's command-line adapter declares the file and gets it back tagged, and a second parser here would have its own opinion.
- `test/contracts/test-design.contract.json`, fifteen oracles, and a replay corpus of eleven stored documents under `test/replay/test-design/` that re-derive every score with no model call. `test/test-contract-oracles.js` and `test/test-eval-replay.js` each grew an arm for the suite, and `test/test-probe-targets.js` grew an end-to-end section that drives the harness against a stub with no model call: a correct run of both sets exits 0, a run that edits the staged epic exits 1 naming fixture mutations, a run that wrote nothing exits 2 as a missing artifact, and three measured quality failures exit 1 naming the metric each one moved.
- `test/probes/test-design.probes.json`, sixteen probes, one per oracle plus a gameability probe that carries a correct run's vocabulary and a generic register's grounding, so it satisfies every seeded-set oracle while reporting nothing the epic supports. Its rationale says plainly that nothing in the contract rejects it and the row-scoped scorer is what does. The corpus is scored rather than only generated: `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` builds its suite list from a hardcoded pair plus auto-discovery of fragment selection, so a new top-level corpus was byte-checked and never reached the scorer, and `testDesignEvidence` closes that. What the scoring reports is null strength across all three classes, and two independent things produce it. All sixteen probes are refused at the qualification gate with `condition-artifact-channel-contract-local`, the same refusal `trace`'s four probes carry. Fourteen of the sixteen also fail pre-flight with `seeded-fault-fired` and exit 3 before any verdict exists; P-011 and P-016 are the two that clear pre-flight, where all four of `trace`'s do. A null rate is the truthful measurement for a corpus whose conditions are local to an artifact channel the contract owns; the corpus does satisfy two coverage rules `trace`'s leaves unsatisfied.
- `test/lib/conformance-counts.js`, one accessor every conformance arm TEA runs reads its expected outcome count through. `eval-quality` publishes `CONFORMANCE_OUTCOME_COUNTS` and the arms grow: the command-probe arm was fifteen assertions when TEA first ran it and is sixteen now, so a transcribed number would have gone on passing while the suite ran an assertion TEA never looked at. Reading it by subscript closed most of that and left one gap. `CONFORMANCE_OUTCOME_COUNTS['command-probe']` for an arm the package has stopped publishing is `undefined`, and the failure read "the suite produced 16 outcome(s) and a complete command-probe run is undefined", which is a true statement about a count and the wrong statement about what happened: the arm was renamed or withdrawn and the reader is sent to audit an assertion list that is fine. A missing entry now fails with the arm named and the arms the package does publish listed, and `test/test-port-totality.js` drives all three of the accessor's failures rather than describing them: an arm the package does not publish, a registry it no longer publishes at all, and an entry present with a count that is not a positive whole number. Those three are kept apart because each sends a reader somewhere different, and collapsing any two produces a message that contradicts itself; an earlier draft answered a present-but-undefined entry with "publishes no outcome count for the \"command-probe\" arm. It publishes command-probe." The live-source check that holds a running arm's file to reading its count from the registry now accepts the accessor alone. The bare subscript also reads the package and was admissible until this requirement gained its second clause: a subscript cannot name the arm when the entry goes, so leaving it admissible would let every arm added later satisfy the gate while producing the message the requirement forbids.
- `npm run test:eval-quality-corpus` compiles `eval-quality`'s own published corpus against the pinned release, before any TEA artifact is asked to run on it.
  TEA's every other check feeds the package TEA's own bytes, so a package regression and a bad migration read identically, and this upgrade moved 31 probes, 10 contracts and both command policies at once.
  This check feeds the package nothing of TEA's. The `./corpus/*` subpath ships `corpus/dev/index.json`, which names 27 entries with a `sha256:` digest each: 24 contracts under `contracts/`, the compile-and-seal example's contract and its sealed brief, and the corpus README. The index also records the failure code each of the three deliberately-refused contracts raises, and it carries no digest of itself, which is the one corpus file it cannot list.
  So the corpus states the expected result and the package computes the observed one. Measured against 3.0.0: 27 entry digests match the shipped bytes, 22 contracts compile, 3 are refused with the code the index records, and the sealed brief the example produces is byte for byte the shipped `brief.json`.
  It also holds the resolved version against the exact pin in `package.json`, so a tree that is not the tree the repository declares fails here rather than somewhere downstream.
  Exit 1 is a moved status, a moved digest or a moved brief; exit 2 is a package, subpath or index that could not be read, an index naming no contract, or a tree resolving a version other than the pin. So an unresolvable install, an empty corpus and a tree nobody installed are each reported as nothing measured rather than as a package regression.
- `npm run test:port-totality` holds TEA's branches against the port vocabularies the installed package declares.
  `ProbeRequest` and `ProbeObservation` are three-member unions tagged by `kind` and the conformance surface publishes six arms, and TEA is CommonJS with no typechecker, so no compiler and no reviewer will notice the day a fourth member arrives: the failure mode is code that keeps working on the members it knows.
  Every member is now handled or declined with the reason recorded, every arm is run or recorded with the reason no check runs it, and a member or arm in neither fails the check by name.
  `probeCommand` narrows every observation to the `cli` member and raises a named error otherwise, which the check proves by calling the narrowing with the two members TEA declines.
  Three of the four harnesses reach the port through `probeCommand`; `test/eval-contract-strength.js` caches every observation itself, so it narrows at its own boundary, on the live answer and on the cached one.
  The narrowing throws outside the fault handler on purpose: a member TEA cannot read is a defect in TEA, and classifying it as an environment failure would file that defect as a lost run.

- `bmad-tea` has a behavioral eval suite and is no longer declared `deferred` in `test/evals/suite-manifest.json`. It was the only TEA skill with no eval of any kind, and the one thing it does is the step every workflow runs after: turn a sentence a user wrote into one item on its ten-item menu. A wrong route is the most expensive mistake the module can make and the cheapest one to miss, because every artifact downstream of it is well-formed, cites the repository, and answers a question nobody asked. `test/eval-bmad-tea-routing.js` puts eighteen intents to the skill through `cli/routing-runner.js`, a new `read-only` command registered as `tea-routing-runner`: ten with a single right menu item, four where two or more items are genuinely close and the right answer is to ask, and four nothing on the menu serves. Each ambiguous intent is ambiguous from what the agent is actually shown, which is the message plus `SKILL.md` and `customize.toml`; an intent whose second reading only opens once you have read a workflow's frontmatter is not ambiguous to the agent, and scoring it as a clarification would measure a fact it was never given.
- Two of the three answers are Step 8 of `src/agents/bmad-tea/SKILL.md` verbatim and the third is not, which is worth stating rather than glossing. Step 8 says to dispatch a clear match directly and to pause only when two or more items are genuinely close, so `route` and `clarify` are the skill's own rules. Its third clause says that when nothing on the menu fits the agent should just continue the conversation, and names chat, clarifying questions and `bmad-help` as fair game: it forbids a dispatch and asks for no refusal. The requirement that an unservable intent be declined and that the answer say what is missing is the suite's own exit condition, and the prompt states it outright, so the skill is scored against a rule it was handed rather than one inferred from text that does not carry it. The skill itself is not edited.
- Five metrics and four counts, and three of the counts are the controls. `routeAccuracy`, `reasonTokenRecall`, `scopeFidelity`, `clarifyRecall` and `declineRecall` measure the decision, the stated reason, the scope the user named, the clarification and the decline. `maxConfidentRoutesOnUnservable`, `maxConfidentRoutesOnAmbiguous` and `maxUnroutedClearIntents` are all zero and they close the cells recall leaves open: a skill that guesses on everything fails the first two, and one that asks about everything fails the third, which Step 8 rules out by name as a confirmation ritual. `maxUnstableCases` is two of eighteen at two repetitions. Every threshold sits on a value its denominator can actually produce, because a threshold between two reachable values is not the gate it reads as, and every one of them except stability has a stored replay case that fails it and nothing else. Stability cannot have one, since it is a property of a pair of repetitions and a stored case is one reply; what is pinned instead is the signature the live run measures it with.
- The stated reason is scored by token containment and never by judgment, and every token has to be a word of the user's own message. The harness refuses one that is not, and that rule is what makes the number mean anything: the prompt asks the agent to name the deciding feature in the message's own words, so a token reachable only from the menu row it is being scored for choosing could be satisfied by echoing that row without reading the message, and a token in neither place would make the threshold unreachable for a skill doing exactly what it was told. `test/eval-bmad-tea-routing.js` owns the one pattern rule and `tools/generate-contracts.js` writes the same pattern sources into the contracts, so `npm run test:contract-oracles` compares an oracle and a scorer that cannot spell it two ways. Scope is held to containment and to a length bound together, because the same prompt asks for the scope in the message's own words and echoing the whole message would otherwise satisfy any token drawn from it.
- The answers never reach the agent. `test/fixtures/tea-routing-eval/intents.json` holds the eighteen messages and is the only file the prompt carries; `ground-truth.json` holds the expected action, menu code, workflow, deciding tokens, scope tokens and clarification candidates. Every assembled prompt is searched for each ground-truth-only key and for the file's own bytes before a model call is spent, and that check runs under `--validate-only`, so it is in the pull-request gate rather than only in the runs that cost money. Every expected menu code is checked against the live `src/agents/bmad-tea/customize.toml`, so editing the menu fails the corpus instead of quietly invalidating it.
- Seventeen stored replies under `test/replay/bmad-tea-routing/` score with no model call, and they are the only part of the suite the deterministic gate can run end to end. They cover a correct route, a wrong menu code, a route whose scope is the whole message copied back, a grounded route with an ungrounded reason, a route that dropped the epic the user named, a `GATE` dispatch that named a workflow behind a menu item whose target is a prompt, a route whose reason is written the way a person writes rather than as a list of the fixture's tokens, a clarification that names nothing to choose between, one that names all three candidates, one that names both by menu label and never by code, a confident route on a genuinely ambiguous intent, a confident route on an unservable one, a decline that says what is missing and one that does not, a clear intent left unrouted, an answer inside a fenced block, and a reply carrying no answer at all. Every stored result was derived by hand.
- Two eval contracts and two probe corpora, generated. The suite is expressed as `tea-routing-intents.contract.json` and `tea-routing-controls.contract.json` because `eval-quality`'s AD-39 scripting bound caps an interaction plan at sixteen steps and the corpus is eighteen intents, one step each; the manifest's `contracts` field is an array for exactly this. Both bind standard input as a literal prompt per step, which is what tells eighteen steps declaring one operation apart, and what the pair costs in bytes. Seventy-two oracles between them read the action, the menu code, the workflow behind it, the stated reason, the scope and the clarifying question, and every one of them was seen resolving false on some answer as well as true on another. The probe corpora carry a gameability probe and a clean control each and no defect probe: a defect probe needs a controlled mutation of the system under test, and the system here is the skill, which this suite must not edit.
- A behavioral eval suite for `bmad-testarch-nfr`, which discharges the deferred entry that said an unsupported PASS could not be told from a grounded one. `test/fixtures/nfr-eval/` carries two evidence bundles with an independently authored ground truth: one whose security evidence meets every stated threshold, whose reliability evidence breaches one, whose performance has a healthy load test and no target anywhere to judge it against, and whose stated 80% coverage threshold has no report behind it; and a clean control whose four domains all pass. The two temptations are the point. Both look like thorough evidence, and a run that supplies its own performance target or reads the tech spec's "well covered by unit and integration tests" as a coverage measurement has produced an unsupported PASS. `maxUnsupportedPass` is the headline metric and its ceiling is zero, independent of `domainStatusAccuracy`, which admits one wrong judgment of eight.
- `cli/nfr-runner.js` and the `tea-nfr-runner` bin, the eval-only command the suite probes through eval-quality's command-line adapter. It is `cli/trace-runner.js` with one artifact instead of two: a prompt on standard input, an agent run in the working directory, the workflow's `nfr-assessment.md` left on disk, and the shared exit-code table so all five TEA commands spell one failure class with one number. `test/lib/probe-targets.js` registers it, which is what authorizes it before a process starts and caps its output. That authorization permits the vendor names and nothing else, which `eval-quality` 3.0.0 requires with no default, and `npm run test:probe-targets` holds the list equal to the one `nfr.contract.json` declares in both directions. The same check drives the command through the real adapter against `test/fixtures/nfr-runner/stub-agent.js`: the report read back at the registry's default path and at the per-run path a staged workspace supplies, an artifact the run never wrote coming back `absent`, and a vendor that exits nonzero classified as a transport failure. Without that the command would be registered, named by a contract, and executed by nothing in the gate.
- `test/contracts/nfr.contract.json`, generated like the other thirteen, and fifteen stored runs under `test/replay/nfr/` with their hand-derived results. Every expected domain status in the ground truth names the rule in the workflow that produces it, and `npm run test:eval-nfr-data` fails when a cited section is gone from the file it names. The suite's own thresholds, case count and runner capabilities are checked against the harness by `npm run test:eval-schemas`, its oracles against the harness scorer by `npm run test:contract-oracles`, and its stored results against the parser by `npm run test:eval-replay`.
- `test/probes/nfr.probes.json`, the probe corpus `nfr.contract.json` shipped without. Every other contract had one, so it was green having never been scored. Three defect probes, every field of them read out of the ground truth: the two domains it flags undecidable, performance because no source states a threshold and maintainability because the evidence for a stated one is absent, and the reliability domain whose evidence breaches a threshold the bundle does state. One clean control from the other bundle. Every one pre-flights, including AD-10's seeded-fault scoping, because the contract's two sensitivity witness legs audit the clean bundle and the manifestation relation is false there. All three defect probes are then refused by AD-9's qualification gate as `condition-artifact-channel-contract-local`, which is the refusal `trace` already carries and for the same reason: the only artifact is a document the contract owns, and the exit code that would qualify is 0 on every completed audit. `test/probes/expected-strength.json` records null strength across all three classes, which is the honest reading of a corpus whose signatures the gate refuses.
- A behavioral eval suite for `bmad-testarch-ci`, which discharges the deferred entry that said the generated pipeline was never parsed or linted, so a syntactically invalid workflow would pass unnoticed. `test/fixtures/ci-eval/` carries two Node projects, each with a written pipeline request at `docs/ci-requirements.md`: one production-shaped request naming thirteen elements a real pipeline carries, and a minimal request naming five and forbidding the rest by name. The minimal project is the control that stops the suite scoring well by emitting everything, the way the shipped template does when adaptation is skipped. The generated `.github/workflows/test.yml` is read through the `yaml` package in strict mode and linted with `actionlint` under pinned flags (`-no-color -format '{{json .}}' -shellcheck= -pyflakes=`); `maxParseFailures` and `maxLintFindings` are both zero. Every requested element is then checked structurally against the parsed document — a trigger by its event and branches or cron, a permission by its scope and level, a command as a standalone invocation, a gate by its own shape (lint gating the test jobs, a four-shard matrix with `fail-fast: false`, a ten-iteration burn-in), an artifact upload by its path, its failure condition and its retention, and the Node version by whether it resolves from `.nvmrc` — and `unrequestedElements` and `workflowRuleViolations` (no unsafe GitHub context in a `run:` block, no `continue-on-error` on a test step) both carry a ceiling of zero. The run has no shell, so step 1's own command-driven pre-flight checks are recorded as not run rather than executed, and nothing here attests that the fixture's own tests pass.
- `cli/ci-runner.js` and the `tea-ci-runner` bin, the eval-only command the suite probes through eval-quality's command-line adapter. It is `cli/nfr-runner.js` under another name: a prompt on standard input, an agent run in the working directory, the platform's workflow file left on disk, and the shared exit-code table so all seven TEA commands spell one failure class with one number. `test/lib/probe-targets.js` registers it, `npm run test:probe-targets` drives it against `test/fixtures/ci-runner/stub-agent.js` end to end, and the same check spawns the harness itself against the stub to prove a correct pipeline, an unparseable one, one carrying elements nobody requested, one editing a pre-existing project file, and one writing nothing each classify the way the manifest declares.
- `test/contracts/ci.contract.json`, generated like the other fourteen. Its oracles are one substring claim per requested or forbidden element, because the deliverable is a text artifact to this vocabulary even though the harness reads it structurally; each is paired with `workflowMentions`, `test/eval-ci.js`'s own document-global predicate, rather than with the row-scored `checkElement` result, the same idiom `test-design`'s oracles use and for the same reason: pairing an oracle with the harness's real check would make the two agree by coincidence, and pairing it with the function the oracle itself restates makes the agreement true by construction. Twenty-one stored runs under `test/replay/ci/` carry hand-derived results, one per threshold-breaching deviation the harness scores plus three correct pipelines: the full and minimal baselines, and a third that spells every Node version the way the shipped template itself does, a run: step reading `.nvmrc` into a step output that `node-version` then interpolates, which neither baseline exercises. The suite's thresholds, case count and runner capabilities are checked against the harness by `npm run test:eval-schemas`, its oracles against the harness scorer by `npm run test:contract-oracles`, and its stored results against the parser by `npm run test:eval-replay`.
- `test/probes/ci.probes.json`: two requested-element plants on the full project (a missing trigger, a missing permission), one forbidden-element plant on the minimal project (the full-request template's burn-in job), and one clean control. The three defect probes fail pre-flight on `seeded-fault-fired` and exit 3 before any verdict exists, unlike `nfr`'s three, which clear pre-flight and are refused only at AD-9's qualification gate; both are `condition-artifact-channel-contract-local` for the same reason underneath: the deliverable is a file the command wrote, and every completed run exits 0 whatever it wrote.
- `test/eval-ci.js` reads and writes every file whose absence is a real question through `test/lib/file-system-port.js`, the same conversion `#178` applied to the harnesses that existed before it, so a new suite did not ship a year behind the convention it was built next to. `test/test-file-system-port.js` gained a fourth corpus case proving it: the ci ground truth scripted as text that is not JSON, which the harness must report and a direct `fs.readFileSync` would silently validate past.
- Stale counts across `README.md`, `test/README.md`, `test/contracts/README.md`, `test/probes/README.md`, `test/test-probe-corpus.js`, `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md`, `docs/explanation/eval-quality-roadmap.md` and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` that the fifteenth contract and fifteenth probe corpus made stale, re-derived from the tree rather than incremented. `.github/workflows/quality.yaml` installs `actionlint` before the eval-ci suite's live and replay checks run, floating to latest behind the harness's own minimum-version floor rather than a pin; `.prettierignore` and `eslint.config.mjs` both exempt `test/replay/ci/**`, whose stored workflow files are deliberately malformed YAML the suite scores rather than content either tool should reformat or lint.
- A behavioral eval suite for `bmad-testarch-atdd`, architecturally new among TEA's eval suites: every sibling suite scores a static text artifact, and this one executes the scaffold Playwright tests the skill generates and reports what actually happened. `cli/atdd-runner.js` is the vendor-calling generation step, probed through eval-quality's command-line adapter like every other TEA runner; `cli/atdd-red-check.js` is a deterministic, no-vendor executor that activates and runs the generated tests, records each one's real exit path, and diffs a digest of every production file before and after the run, with no knowledge of the story's criteria or declared failure pattern. `test/eval-atdd.js`'s `scoreRun` reads that report against the ground truth and does the judgment `atdd-red-check.js` deliberately leaves out: a story-declared assertion failure scores as correct, while a pass, a load error, a still-skipped test, or a failure for the wrong reason all score as defects, and a test mapping to no supplied acceptance criterion is reported rather than silently counted.
  Because it executes untrusted generated code, it never runs unconfined. `cli/lib/atdd-isolation.js` selects `seatbelt` on macOS and `bubblewrap` on Linux and throws `ISOLATION_UNAVAILABLE` on any other platform or when the platform's backend binary is missing or the kernel refuses to run it; there is no unconfined fallback path. Raw `unshare --net` was rejected first for giving no filesystem confinement at all. The pre-existing `cli/lib/isolate.js` was rejected next: neither of its backends denies network access at all, `(allow default)` in its generated seatbelt profile and no `--unshare-net` in its bwrap invocation, so a generated test under it could still reach the network, and its bwrap backend confines writes only inside the project root rather than the whole filesystem, since the rest of the tree stays bound read-write from its initial `--dev-bind / /`. `atdd-isolation.js`'s two backends deny both network and filesystem writes by default and re-permit only loopback and the declared workspace.
  Two bugs surfaced only by running the sandbox for real. `spawn(..., {stdio: 'ignore'})` threw `EACCES` opening `/bin/sh` under `--unshare-user`, because `'ignore'` opens `/dev/null` and bubblewrap's default `/dev` is empty under that flag; fixed by adding `--dev /dev`. `atdd-red-check.js`'s `NODE_PATH` was set to the project root, but `NODE_PATH` wants the `node_modules` directory itself rather than a starting point to resolve upward from, so the sandboxed Playwright process could not resolve its own dependencies; fixed by passing `path.join(PROJECT_ROOT, 'node_modules')`.
  `.github/workflows/quality.yaml`'s `validate` job installs `bubblewrap` via `apt-get` immediately before the isolation proof, since it is not preinstalled on the `ubuntu-latest` runner image and a failed install now surfaces there rather than three steps later as an opaque `ISOLATION_UNAVAILABLE`.
  Deliberately out of scope: the probe corpus (`tools/generate-probes.js` / `test/lib/probe-scoring.js`) has no atdd wiring, and `npm test` is green without it. This is rigor beyond the story's literal acceptance criteria, not a gap in them.
- Adversarial review of the atdd suite above, in the same pull request, found that `cli/atdd-red-check.js` and `cli/atdd-runner.js` were never actually run by any automated check: `npm test` only replayed a hand-captured report and validated static corpus shape, and the `mutate` `STUB_MODE` built into `test/fixtures/atdd-runner/stub-agent.js` had nothing wired to invoke it. `test/test-probe-targets.js`'s `checkAtddHarnessSmoke` now drives `test/eval-atdd.js` end to end against that stub, the way its three sibling harnesses already are: a correct generation, executed for real under the real isolation backend, meets every threshold; a generation that edits a file under `src/` is caught as a quality failure naming the production mutation; a generation that writes no scaffold does not pass. This is also what proves `cli/atdd-red-check.js` refuses an empty scaffold directory (new: it previously exited 0 with 0% coverage rather than the exit 2 its own docstring promised) and that its and `cli/atdd-runner.js`'s declared runner capabilities still agree, checked the same way the other three runners already are.
  `cli/lib/atdd-net-guard.cjs`, the in-process half of isolation, had the same gap: nothing loaded it and exercised it directly. `test/test-atdd-net-guard.js` now proves all of its refusals in isolation from the OS backend: `net.Socket#connect`, `dns.lookup`, `dns.promises.lookup`, every c-ares resolver method, `dns.lookupService` (added: the reverse of `dns.lookup`, resolving through the same OS path and previously unguarded), `dgram` send with an explicit address, `dgram` connect-then-send with none (added: a connected UDP socket's `send()` carries no address argument, so it read past the existing address-only check), and the `TEST_WORKER_INDEX`-gated child-process refusal, each against a real non-loopback attempt and a real loopback one.
  Also found and fixed: `test/test-atdd-isolation.js`'s credential-seal check derived the marker path it probed for from `process.env.HOME`, which the sandbox always sets to the workspace, so the check could not fail regardless of what the sandbox actually confined; it now asserts `HOME` by exact equality and separately proves, rather than assumes, that a real credential path known outright (not derived from `$HOME`) is still readable, since neither backend denies reads. Its hardcoded connection target moved to `192.0.2.1` (RFC 5737 TEST-NET-1) so the check depends on no third party's host staying up. The fixture server `cli/atdd-red-check.js` starts is now spawned `detached` and stopped by killing its whole process group: `npm start` forked `node src/server.js` as its own child, so killing only the top PID left that process holding the port after the run, measured live. `startServer`'s readiness loop also now treats a process killed by signal the same as one that exited, which it previously missed. `--unshare-pid`, `--unshare-ipc` and `--die-with-parent` were tried on the bubblewrap invocation, verified against the full isolation proof and a real end-to-end run in a `ubuntu:24.04` Docker container, and then reverted: the actual `ubuntu-latest` GitHub Actions runner refused to bring up loopback with them added (`bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR: Operation not permitted`), a restriction the Docker container did not reproduce. Not attempted again in this PR.
  That revert did not fix CI: the same failure recurred with the flag set already verified live before it, which is what showed the actual cause was never the added flags. Ubuntu 24.04+ ships `kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=1`, which blocks `bwrap --unshare-net` from configuring its own loopback interface inside an unprivileged user namespace unless the `bwrap` binary itself carries an AppArmor profile granting `userns`, one this runner's `apt-get install bubblewrap` does not provide. A widely hit issue on `ubuntu-latest` runners (Claude Code's own sandboxed Bash tool, Codex CLI and others report the identical message), not something a plain container reproduces, because container engines commonly run without AppArmor confinement active inside the container at all. `.github/workflows/quality.yaml`'s `validate` and `cli` jobs now disable the restriction (`sudo sysctl -w kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=0`) immediately after installing bubblewrap; this governs whether `bwrap` can create the namespace at all, not what `cli/lib/atdd-isolation.js` does once it exists, which is everything this suite actually tests. The `cli` job needed the same fix for an unrelated reason: `test/eval-all.js`, which `test:cli` spawns for real, preflights every suite the manifest declares, atdd included now that it is no longer `deferred`, so any job that runs `eval-all.js` end to end needs a working isolation backend even when the suite under direct test is a different one entirely. The seatbelt profile's blanket `(allow network-outbound (remote unix-socket))` is removed, since it permitted a connection to any local unix socket, including a live `ssh-agent` or Docker daemon socket, and a real end-to-end run against the fixture still completes without it. `cli/atdd-red-check.js`'s digest exclusions now also skip `_bmad/`, matching the generation-phase scope in `test/eval-atdd.js`, so an identical write there is no longer scored as a defect in one phase and ignored in the other. A generation-phase production mutation is now reported by its changed path rather than by the fixed string `'(a declared production file changed)'`. `runCase` now also checks the real repository's working tree around the execution phase, the same way it already did around generation, so a sandbox that silently stopped confining would not be the one and only thing standing between generated test code and the real repository.
  `scoreRun` also scored a "red for the intended reason" verdict on message text alone: Playwright's JSON reporter carries no field separating an `expect()` matcher failure from a plain thrown `Error`, verified live across `toBe`, `toEqual` and `toContain`, so a criterion's `declaredPattern` (usually the literal "Expected: X" / "Received: Y" text an assertion prints) could be satisfied by a scaffold that never made a real assertion at all. It now also requires the message to carry the `expect(` marker every real matcher failure prints, which every stored replay fixture's message already does. `test/test-atdd-scoring.js` proves the requirement directly on synthetic reports.
  `test/test-atdd-isolation.js`'s privilege check read `process.getuid()` on the harness's own outer process and never on the sandboxed one; `--unshare-user` builds a new user namespace, and one that happened to remap the real uid to 0 inside it would still pass. It now also reads the uid from inside the sandbox directly, verified live to still read the real, non-root uid on both seatbelt and a real Linux container.
  CodeRabbit's review of this pull request found and fixed, each verified live: `.gitignore`'s earlier fix for the one tracked file under the ignored `_bmad-output` only satisfied `git check-ignore` on the file itself, not `git add`, which still reported it ignored because an ancestor directory entry matched; the entry is now `/*` with a negation at every level, verified against a real `git add --dry-run`. The production-mutation digest compared file content alone, so a scaffold that flipped a file's executable bit with no byte changed went undetected; both phases now snapshot the permission bits alongside the content. `startServer` trusted whatever answered the fixture's health URL without confirming it was the process it just spawned, so a stray process already on the port could be silently reused; it now rejects an already-occupied URL before spawning. The fixture server's locker route let a malformed `%` in the URL throw an uncaught `URIError` and crash the whole process; it now answers 400. `test/test-atdd-isolation.js`'s home-marker write ran outside any handler, so an unwritable home would abort the whole proof with a stack trace instead of a named failure; wrapped now. `test/test-atdd-net-guard.js`'s two dgram checks only inspected stdout, so a child that hung past its timeout could still read as a pass; both now also require `status === 0`. `test/eval-atdd.js`'s `validateCorpus` comment claimed a live check on the fixture's AC-5 route that the function never performs; reworded to state what is actually asserted. My own `checkAtddHarnessSmoke`'s "nothing written" case accepted any non-zero exit; tightened to the exact exit code and failure class the run actually produces. `test/test-eval-replay.js` bypassed its own `readJson` helper for the stored atdd report, so a malformed file would throw instead of reporting a controlled corpus error; fixed. Its atdd replay also dropped `usesTestSkip` from the projected result entirely and carried no digest of the scoring-relevant ground truth, unlike every sibling suite, so a report that lost its `test.skip()` marker or a criterion whose id, pattern or order moved could both replay clean; `SCORER_VERSION` moves to 10 for the first and a `scoringInputsDigest` guard, verified live against a real ground-truth edit, closes the second. The atdd contract's sensitivity witness had three gaps: its `citedFiles` omitted the witness story itself, so an edit to it left the generated contract stale; the generation prompt told the agent nothing outside the project root was relevant while also pointing `{story_file}` outside it for the witness leg, a direct contradiction; and the witness leg's relation only required _absence_ of the corpus criterion's id, which an empty or unrelated scaffold also satisfies, so it now also requires _presence_ of the witness story's own criterion id. README.md and `test/README.md` gained the `atdd` suite in the `eval:all` description and the `bubblewrap` prerequisite `npm test` needs on Linux, both previously undocumented. One finding was evaluated and left as is: keeping the probe-scoring's `run-measured` oracle scorer untested on its false side matches the identical, deliberate pattern every sibling suite already uses for that same oracle kind in this file, so testing atdd's alone would be a new inconsistency, not a fix for one.
- A recorded `eval:all` run can now be compared against the last one this repository stored (TEA Story 5.2, stage one: no live agent call is spent by anything in this change). Two gaps in what a run summary carried are closed first: `contractVersions[path]` was always `null` by omission because nothing populated it, and no record named the installed `eval-quality` version at all. `test/lib/contract-versions.js`'s `contractVersionsFor(suite)` reads each of a suite's declared `test/contracts/*.contract.json` files off disk and returns its `schemaVersion` (or `null` for a missing or malformed contract, rather than throwing); all nine `test/eval-*.js` harnesses now pass it into their own `suiteResultRecord({...})` call, so `contracts[].version` is a real stamp in every future record. `runSummaryRecord` (`test/lib/eval-record.js`) now stamps `evalQualityVersion` from the installed package's own `VERSION` export. `test/schema/eval-result.js`'s `evalRunSchema` gained the required `evalQualityVersion` field and `SCHEMA_VERSION` moves to 1.3.0, `test/schema/eval-result.schema.json` regenerated to match.
  `test/lib/compare-eval-runs.js`'s `compareEvalRuns(previous, current)` reports what changed between two stored runs, suite by suite: a `failureClass` change, a measurement change, and a suite present in one run and not the other. It refuses distinctly, naming the reason, when the two runs carry a different `evalQualityVersion` or a suite common to both declares different thresholds or repetitions; a suite present in only one of the two runs is named as added or removed, since Epic 6 adds suites one at a time. For a suite whose stored result carries `{outcomes, strength, comparabilityKey}`, the comparison calls `test/lib/compare-dominance.js`'s `compareStoredResults` (TEA Story 5.1) directly, proven by `test/test-compare-eval-runs.js` constructing that shape directly and confirming the call fires.
  `tools/record-eval-run.js --from <path>` is the wiring stage two runs: it loads the run-summary JSON `eval:all --json <path>` already wrote, compares it against `test/results/eval-all/latest.json` if one is stored, prints the comparison, and writes the run to `latest.json` and to a timestamped `test/results/eval-all/history/<instant>.json` through the same `writeRunSummary` `eval-all.js` itself uses (so a scripted-clock run is refused here too). `test/results/eval-all/` is a new git-committed directory, carrying only a `README.md` until stage two actually runs a live agent through it.
  `test/test-compare-eval-runs.js` covers every row of the story's I/O matrix with hand-built fixtures: no prior run, no drift, a regression, an added suite, a removed suite, an `eval-quality`-version refusal, a suite-configuration refusal, and a contract file with no `schemaVersion`. `test:compare-eval-runs` joins the `npm test` chain (right after `test:compare-dominance`) and `.github/workflows/quality.yaml`'s `validate` job in the same position.
- TEA's `eval-quality` devDependency moves from 3.0.0 to 3.4.0 across four pin bumps recorded individually above: 3.1.0 carries the schema-version constants, `compareDominance` and the `eval-quality-gates` binary; 3.2.0 publishes `lockfile-age.exclude`, `licences.undeclared` and `lockfile-age.cache` in their final shapes; 3.3.0 publishes `resolveCheck` and its evidence-resolution helpers off the top-level entry point and fixes the `dependency-direction` method-shorthand false positive; 3.4.0 adds `dated.claims[].asOf`, the doc-claims staleness pin. Eight `eval-quality-gates` consumer gates are adopted end to end, each behind its own `npm run test:*` script: `doc-counts`, `doc-claims` and `doc-invocations` hold every published count, prose claim and fenced command in `docs/` and `README.md` against the source that computes it; `dependency-direction` runs at zero violations rather than report-only; `package-boundary`, `field-ownership`, `licences` and `lockfile-age` are new outright.
